Shifted quantum groups via critical stable envelopes
Yalong Cao, Andrei Okounkov, Yehao Zhou, Zijun Zhou

Abstract
. Given a symmetric quiver with potential, we develop a geometric construction of shifted Yangians acting on the critical cohomologies of antidominantly framed quiver varieties with extended potentials, using the R R -matrices constructed from critical stable envelopes. We relate such Reshetikhin type Yangians to Drinfeld type Yangians arising from critical cohomological Hall algebras. Several detailed examples, including the trivial, Jordan, and tripled Jordan quivers are explicitly computed. For symmetric quiver varieties with potentials, by using the smallness property of their affinization maps, we derive explicit formulas for quantum multiplication by divisors in terms of Casimir elements of the associated Lie (super)algebras, extending results from Nakajima quiver varieties to the critical setting. A similar formula in the antidominantly framed case is also obtained, which includes Hilbert schemes of points on ℂ 3 \mathbb{C}^{3} as examples.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification

Primary
14N35, 17B37. Secondary
16G20, 22E99

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Overview

This paper is the second in our sequence of papers devoted to
the theory and applications of stable envelopes for critical
loci. The opening paper
[
COZZ1
]
of the sequence contains
a detailed introduction with which we assume the reader is
familiar.

The bulk of this paper is dedicated to the construction
of the
shifted quantum group
actions on the
critical
cohomology
along the lines described
in
[
COZZ1
, §1.8, §1.9]

1
1

1

The case using critical
K
-theory will be pursued in
[
COZZ3
]
.
, see §
3
and §
4
.
It builds on the ideas that go back to
[
FRT
]
, in a
special case of a classical Lie algebra in its defining representation, and to
[
R
]
, in general.

Concretely, the critical stable envelopes

\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w})\to H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w}),\quad\operatorname{Stab}^{\mathsf{s}}_{\mathfrak{C}}\colon K^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w})\to K^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})

make the relevant critical
cohomology or
K
-theory groups into a tensor
category with a meromorphic braiding, called the

R
-
matrix
, see §
2
. After that, the operators of the quantum group
appear as matrix elements of the

R
-matrix.

The full quantum group structure also involves the
antipode operation
,
related to the notions of left and right duals,
{}^{*}V
and
V^{*}
, for
a representation
V
of a quantum group.
Over a field, which means equivariant
localization to the fixed locus of an automorphism
g
in the geometric context,
the underlying vector space of both
{}^{*}V
and
V^{*}
is
the ordinary vector space dual of
V
. Here
V
stands for a localized
critical cohomology group and the choice of
g
incorporates both the
value of the continuous parameters for the shifted Yangian and
the parameters of the representation, as is the common practice in
many other geometric representation theory contexts, see
[
CG
]
for
a comprehensive discussion. We identify the dual of
V

with
V
itself using the standard
bilinear form
on critical cohomology,
which exists and is nondegenerate when the
g
-fixed critical locus
\mathrm{Crit}(\mathsf{w})^{g}
is
proper, see §
6.2
. This induces a transposition

anti-automorphism
on the Yangian, discussed in §
6.3
.
In Reshetikhin’s reconstruction
[
R
]
,
R
-matrices of the
dual representations appear as the inverse of the transpose of
R
in one
of the factors. This completes the construction of the quantum group.

An important application of the invariant bilinear form is a geometric
criterion of the
irreducibility
of the Yangian modules. Recall that
we package the continuous parameters of the algebra and of the module
into a choice of an automorphism
g
and work in equivariant cohomology
localized at
g
. Under fairly
general hypotheses, we prove in Theorem
7.3
that our critical
cohomology modules are
cogenerated
by the vacuum under
the action of the Yangian
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}
. In fact, it is enough to consider the subalgebra

\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}^{\leqslant}\subset\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}
generated by
the lowering
operators
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}^{-}
and the operators
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}^{0}
of the
cup product with tautological classes. If, in addition,
\mathrm{Crit}(\mathsf{w})^{g}
is
proper, and hence a nondegenerate invariant bilinear form exists,
one can conclude that the Yangian module is irreducible, see Proposition

7.6
.

In §
5
, we prove a formula for the
quantum
multiplication by divisors
, first for a symmetric
quiver variety with potential, and then in the antidominantly
shifted case, see
[
COZZ1
, §1.11]
and
below for additional introductory material.
We recall from
[
COZZ1
]
that an antidominant shift means that
we allow asymmetry in the framing dimension vectors,
but require
\mathbf{d}_{\textup{in}}\geqslant\mathbf{d}_{\textup{out}}
.
The sign of the inequality here is correlated with the
specific stability condition on which we focus. Namely,
we consider a framed quiver representation stable if the
the maps out of the framing spaces and the maps between
the unframed spaces generate everything. It remains
an interesting question to explore what happens for
other shifts and other stability conditions.

§
8
, §
9
, and §
10
are devoted to making our general
theory explicit and application-ready in several
fundamental examples, which we explain in more details below.

1.2.
Reshetikhin Yangian v.s. Drinfeld Yangian

We recall that in the formalism of
[
R
]
, quantum group
operators appear as matrix elements of
R
-matrices and
the relations among them come from the morphisms in the
corresponding category, that is, from maps that intertwine

R
-matrices. For example, the Yang-Baxter equation itself
results in the RTT=TTR relation of
[
FRT
]
.

For Yangians
of finite-dimensional Lie algebras, this recovers Drinfeld
Yangians. In its “new” presentation, Drinfeld Yangian is generated
by currents corresponding to the simple positive and negative
roots and polynomial loops into the Cartan subalgebra. In the
geometric situation, the latter correspond to operators of the
cup product by the tautological characteristic classes. The
former have to do with the locus of extensions by a representation
of the unframed quiver in which all maps are zero and the dimension
vector is a
\delta
-function.

We adapt the construction of the
Drinfeld type Yangian to our setting in §
3
, building on
the works of Nakajima
[
Nak1
]
, Neguţ
[
N
]
,
Varagnolo and Vasserot
[
VV1
,
VV2
]
, and Yang and Zhao
[
YZ1
]
,
among other works in the really vast literature that constructs
Yangian-like algebras using Hall algebras of Schiffmann-Vasserot
[
SV1
]
and
Kontsevich-Soibelman type
[
KS
]
.

Theorem 1.1
.

(Theorems
3.7
,
3.21
,
4.8
)
Let
Q
be a symmetric quiver with potential
\mathsf{W}
, and
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}^{Q_{0}}
.

•

There is a Drinfeld type
\mu
-shifted Yangian

\mathcal{D}\widetilde{\mathcal{SH}}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})
defined by the double of spherical nilpotent critical CoHAs, which acts on the equivariant critical cohomology of framed quiver varieties with potentials by Hecke type correspondences specified in Theorem
3.7
.

•

When
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}
, there is a Reshetikhin type
\mu
-shifted Yangian

\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})
defined by geometric
R
-matrices
which come from critical stable envelopes. It naturally acts on equivariant critical cohomology of

\mu=\mathbf{d}_{\textup{out}}-\mathbf{d}_{\textup{in}}
antidominant-framed quiver varieties with potentials.

•

When
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}
, the action of
\mathcal{D}\widetilde{\mathcal{SH}}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})
factors through
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})
via a natural map

\mathcal{D}\widetilde{\mathcal{SH}}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})\to\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W}).

From the technical point of view, to define Reshetikhin type shifted Yangians for symmetric quivers with potentials, we need to consider a so-called

auxiliary data set

\mathcal{C}
, which specifies framings, extensions of torus actions and potentials to the framed quivers, see Definition
4.1
. For a choice of
\mathcal{C}
, one can define
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})

(Definition
4.6
) and the Reshetikhin type
\mu
-shifted Yangian is defined with respect to the union of all auxiliary data sets:

\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W}):=\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\bigcup_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}).

Apriori, this seems not possible to manipulate due to the possible infinite number of auxiliary data. But remarkably, we show that
if
\mathcal{C}
is
admissible
, which requires certain torus weight condition on the framings, see Definition
4.1
, we have
the following.

Theorem 1.2
.

(Theorem
4.44
)
If
\mathcal{C}
is admissible, then for any
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}
, we have
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})=\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})
.

This makes computations manageable as one can for example choose a finite set of auxiliary data labelled by the vertex of quiver
Q
.
We constantly used it in computations throughout the paper.

1.3.
PBW type theorem and Lie subalgebra
\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}

To control the size of our zero shifted Yangian
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
, we need the following
PBW
type theorem.

Theorem 1.3
.

(Proposition
4.23
, Theorems
4.26
,
4.45
)
Let
Q
be a symmetric quiver with potential
\mathsf{W}
.

•

There is a Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}\subset\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
defined from the classical
R
-matrix.

•

\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
is generated by
\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
and multiplication by tautological classes.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}\>\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})\cong\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}[u])\,,

with respect to the filtration by degree in
u
.

•

When
Q
is a tripled quiver and
\mathsf{W}
is the canonical cubic potential,
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
recovers the Maulik-Okounkov Yangian
and
\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
recovers the Maulik-Okounkov Lie algebra
[
MO
]
.

To study the nonzero shifted Yangians, one can use the (parametrized) shifted homomorphisms in §
4.5

to relate them to the zero shifted case.

Note that in general, the Reshetikhin Yangian is much larger than the Drinfeld type Yangian.
We conjecture
that, on the CoHA side, the right object to consider is
the BPS Lie algebra of Davison–Meinhardt
[
DM
]
. Namely, in
parallel with a conjecture made by one of us in the context of
Nakajima varieties, and recently proven by Botta and Davison in

[
BD
]
and Schiffmann and Vasserot in
[
SV3
]
, we conjecture the Davison–Meinhardt Lie algebra is
the positive half of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
.

If true, this conjecture provides
a natural collection of quantum group doubles for the cohomological Hall
algebra, indexed by the shift parameter
\mu
. In general, the lack
of properness is the reason why only half of the quantum
group operations are defined in the CoHA setting. From the beginning,
stable envelopes have served as particularly useful canonical extensions of
attracting manifolds to proper correspondences, and it would be
logical for them to have a role here, too.

1.4.
Coproducts

Compared to coproducts studied by many people from the CoHA side, we have a
coproduct on
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})
defined by splitting the
R
-matrix in §
4.2
which should correspond to the
standard coproduct
.
This is justified by the following.

Theorem 1.4
.

(Remark
4.42
, Theorem
4.43
)
Let
A
be a generalized Cartan matrix with symmetrizer
D
,
\mathfrak{g}_{A,D}
be the symmetrizable Kac-Moody Lie algebra associated to the pair
(A,D)
and
Y_{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{A,D})
be its Yangian defined by explicit generators with relations
(Example
3.9
). Let
(\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W})
be the associated tripled quiver with potential of Geiss-Leclerc-Schröer
[
GLS
]
(see Appendix
A.1
).
Then there is an algebra homomorphism

(1.1)

\varrho\colon Y_{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{A,D})_{\mathrm{loc}}\xrightarrow{\text{Ex.\ref{ex on gen symi}}}\mathcal{D}\widetilde{\mathcal{SH}}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W})_{\mathrm{loc}}\xrightarrow{\text{Thm.\ref{thm drinfeld yangian map to rtt yangian}}}\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}).

When
\mathfrak{g}_{A,D}
is a finite dimensional simple Lie algebra,
Y_{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{A,D})_{\mathrm{loc}}
has a standard coproduct due to Drinfeld, then the map
(
1.1
)
is an injective bialgebra homomorphism. Moreover, there is a bialgebra isomorphism

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varrho}\colon Y_{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{A,D})_{\mathrm{loc}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{d}_{i,k}:i\in\widetilde{Q}_{0},\,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}]\cong\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}).

Here
\mathsf{d}_{i,k}
are primitive for all
i
and
k
,
\widetilde{\varrho}
on the first component is
\varrho
, and
\widetilde{\varrho}(\mathsf{d}_{i,k})
is the multiplication by
\mathrm{ch}_{k+1}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i})
operator when acting on a state space with framing vector spaces
\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in}},\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out}}
.

We remark that since Drinfeld
[
Dri
]
wrote down the standard coproduct formula in the case of finite dimensional simple Lie algebras, a proof has never appeared in the literature until the work of Guay-Nakajima-Wendlandt
[
GNW
]
. The above theorem can be seen as another proof using geometric methods (critical stable envelopes).
In fact, the coproduct formula of
[
GNW
]
also works for more general affine Kac-Moody Lie algebras. We expect
the map (
1.1
) preserves coproduct in those cases too.

1.5.
Smallness for symmetric quiver varieties

The theory developed in this sequence of papers is a far-reaching
generalization of what was previously known for Nakajima varieties and
other symplectic resolutions. Historically, the
symplectic form played an important technical role in
various geometric arguments, and a different technical ingredient had
to be found in the more general situation treated here.
The property we use, and which may be of independent interest,
is the following.

Let
X=\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
be a symmetric quiver variety.
We prove in a companion paper
[
COZZ2
]
that
X
has
a Poisson structure such that:

(1)

the Poisson center
\mathcal{O}_{B}\subset H^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
is a polynomial
algebra,

(2)

the fibers of the map
X\to B
have symplectic
singularities, in particular, finitely many symplectic leaves,

(3)

the generic fiber of
X\to B
is affine.

Moreover, if a torus
\mathsf{A}
acts on
X
in a symmetry-preserving way
then
\mathsf{A}
acts trivially on
B
.

As a corollary, the affinization map

X\to X_{0}=\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
is birational and
small
. By
definition, that means that
X\times_{X_{0}}X
is the union of the
diagonal and components of dimension
less
than
\dim X
.
The components of dimension
\dim X-1
appear as

Steinberg correspondences
and have a very important place in
the theory, see §
2.7
.

Smallness
plays a crucial role in our analysis of the
quantum multiplication by divisors for symmetric quiver varieties with
potentials. There, one needs to understand the Steinberg
correspondence
Q_{\beta}
formed, in the sense of the virtual fundamental
class, by pairs of points lying on a rational curve of degree

\beta
. For any choice of attracting directions
\mathfrak{C}
, we prove in
Theorem
5.5

the commutativity of the diagram

(1.2)

\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(F,\mathsf{w})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\pm\mathrm{Q}_{\beta}^{F}}
\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Q}_{\beta}}
\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(F,\mathsf{w})}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})\,,}
\scriptstyle{\,\,(-)|_{F}\circ\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}^{-1}}

for any fixed component
F\subseteq X^{\mathsf{A}}
with a precise determination of the sign and up to an equivariant scalar trivial on
\mathsf{A}
. In the symplectic
situation, the proof of the parallel result in
[
MO
]
rests on the
analysis of broken/unbroken contributions, originated from
[
OP
]
. We replace that analysis by the smallness argument in broader generality of symmetric quivers with potentials.

1.6.
Quantum multiplication by divisors

As in the symplectic case, it is convenient to get rid of the signs in
(
1.2
) and elsewhere by shifting the origin in the
torus of degree-counting variables
z

to a certain point of order
2
. This is called the

modified quantum product
, see Definition
5.9
, and is denoted
by
\widetilde{\star}
in our paper.

Theorem 1.5
.

(Theorem
5.10
, Corollary
5.11
)
Let
X
be a symmetric quiver variety with a potential
\mathsf{w}
.
Suppose that the specialization map
\mathrm{sp}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})\to H^{\mathsf{T}}(X)
is injective, then the modified quantum multiplication by divisor
\lambda
on

H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})
is given by

(1.3)

c_{1}(\lambda)\,\widetilde{\star}\,\cdot=c_{1}(\lambda)\cup\,\cdot-\sum_{\alpha>0}\alpha(\lambda)\frac{z^{\alpha}}{(-1)^{|\alpha|}-z^{\alpha}}\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha}+\cdots\,,

where dots stand for a scalar operator,
the sum is over the positive roots
\alpha
of the Lie superalgebra

\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}\subset\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
underlying the Yangian, and
the Casimir element

\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathfrak{g}_{-\alpha}\subset U(\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}})
is the canonical tensor.

The shape of the
formula (
1.3
) is essentially the same as in the
symplectic case, the main difference is the presence of the parity

(-1)^{|\alpha|}
of a root in a superalgebra. In (
1.3
) we sum over the positive roots because of our choice of
the stability condition, discussed above. For a general
stability condition
\theta
on a symmetric quiver variety the
corresponding range of summation should be modified to

\theta\cdot\alpha>0
.

In general, the formula (
1.3
) holds on the
image of the specialization map from the critical cohomology to
the ordinary cohomology. In the eventuality that the specialization
map is not injective, it is the image group that is the natural
object to consider in the enumerative setup.

For quiver varieties defined from symmetric quivers with
antidominant framing

\mu=\mathbf{d}_{\textup{out}}-\mathbf{d}_{\textup{in}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}
(which include Hilbert schemes of points on
\mathbb{C}^{3}
as examples, see §
10.4
), the formula
(
1.3
) is modified as follows.

Theorem 1.6
.

(Theorem
5.13
)
We have

(1.4)

c_{1}(\lambda)\,\widetilde{\star}\,=c_{1}(\lambda)\cup-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha>0\\
\alpha\cdot\mu=0\end{subarray}}\alpha(\lambda)\frac{z^{\alpha}}{(-1)^{|\alpha|}-z^{\alpha}}\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha>0\\
\alpha\cdot\mu\neq 0\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|\alpha|}\alpha(\lambda)\,z^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha,\mu}+\cdots\,,

where
\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha,\mu}
is a certain shifted Casimir element
in the shifted Yangian
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})
, defined as follows.
As a special case of the
parametrized shift homomorphisms
defined
in §
4.5
, we have a map

\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})\to\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})[z],

which sends
\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha}
to a polynomial of
z
of degree

-\mu\cdot\alpha
. The shifted Casimir element

\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha,\mu}
is the
coefficient of
z^{-\mu\cdot\alpha}
in this polynomial, see
Definition
4.50
.

1.7.
Examples

We apply our general theory to specific examples, and find presentations of our Reshetikhin type shifted Yangians using generators and relations.

Theorem 1.7
.

(Theorems
8.1
,
9.1
,
10.7
)
For an arbitrary
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0}
, we have

•

if
Q
is the trivial quiver, there is a natural algebra isomorphism

\displaystyle Y_{\mu}(\mathfrak{gl}_{1|1})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hbar]}\mathbb{C}(\hbar)\cong\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,0)\>,

where
Y_{\mu}(\mathfrak{gl}_{1|1})
is the

\mu
-shifted Yangian of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1|1}
(Definition
8.2
).

•

if
Q
is the Jordan quiver, there is a natural algebra isomorphism

\displaystyle Y_{\mu}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hbar]}\mathbb{C}(\hbar)\cong\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,0)\>,

where
Y_{\mu}(\mathfrak{gl}_{2})
is the Frassek-Pestun-Tsymbaliuk

\mu
-shifted Yangian of
\mathfrak{gl}_{2}
(Definition
9.11
).

•

if
Q
is the tripled Jordan quiver, equipped with the canonical cubic potential
\mathsf{W}
, there is a surjective algebra homomorphism

\displaystyle\varrho_{\mu}\colon Y_{\mu}(\widehat{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})\twoheadrightarrow\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W}),

where
Y_{\mu}(\widehat{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})
is the
\mu
-shifted affine Yangian of
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
(Definition
10.1
). Moreover,
\varrho_{0}
is a bialgebra isomorphism, with respect to the coproduct on
Y_{0}(\widehat{\mathfrak{gl}}_{1})
defined by Schiffmann-Vasserot
[
SV3
]
, and the coproduct on
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})
defined in §
4.2
using critical stable envelopes.

In the above examples, we also study their representations, including
higher spin, dual Verma and prefundamental modules in the
\mathfrak{gl}_{2}
case (§
9.1
, Theorem
9.28
), Kirillov-Reshetikhin modules, vacuum MacMahon modules, and several modules arising from spiked instantons of Nekrasov in the affine
\mathfrak{gl}_{1}
case which are related to the vertex algebra at the corner
\mathcal{Y}_{L,M,N}
of Gaiotto and Rapčák (Theorem
10.12
, Proposition
10.18
). We perform explicit computations of stable envelopes and
R
-matrices, including braiding between higher spin modules, dual Verma and prefundamental modules §
9.2
, §
9.3
, §
9.8
, as well as some examples in Lie superalgebras, see §
8.1
, §
8.2
, and Appendix
B
.

Statements and Declarations

We have no conflicts of interest to disclose.

2.
R
-matrices

In this section, we define
R
-matrices in the context of critical cohomology on quiver varieties with potentials, using corresponding
Hall (stable) envelopes developed in
[
COZZ1
, §9]
. We study their properties and deduce Yang-Baxter equations. For later use, we also analyze the classical
R
-matrices, and study Steinberg correspondences and their compatibility with stable envelopes.

2.1.
Hall envelopes and stable envelopes

Let
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1})
be a quiver and
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}},\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out}}\in\mathbb{Q}^{Q_{0}}
,
\underline{\mathbf{d}}=(\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}},\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out}})
. Denote the
space of framed representations
of
Q
with gauge dimension
\mathbf{v}
and in-coming framing dimension
\mathbf{d}_{\mathrm{in}}

and out-going framing dimension
\mathbf{d}_{\mathrm{out}}
by

(2.1)

R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}):=R(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})=\bigoplus_{a\in Q_{1}}\underbrace{\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{t(a)}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{h(a)}})}_{X_{a}}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{0}}\left(\underbrace{\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{\mathrm{in},i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{i}})}_{A_{i}}\oplus\underbrace{\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}})}_{B_{i}}\right).

Let
\mathsf{T}\subseteq\operatorname{Aut}_{G}(R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}))
be a subtorus in the flavour group, and
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{T}
be a subtorus. We fix a cyclic stability
\theta\in\mathbb{Q}_{<0}^{Q_{0}}
and define the
quiver variety
as the GIT quotient:

(2.2)

\displaystyle X=\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}):=R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})/\!\!/_{\theta}G=R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{s}/G,

where the
gauge group

G=\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathrm{GL}(\mathbf{v}_{i})
naturally acts on
R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
by compositions with maps.
As there is no relation imposed on the quiver, the above quiver variety is a smooth quasi-projective variety.

Let
\mathfrak{C}
be a chamber associated with
\mathsf{A}
-action on
\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
,
\mathsf{w}\colon X\to\mathbb{C}
be a
\mathsf{T}
-invariant regular function.
Then Hall envelopes

\displaystyle\begin{gathered}\mathrm{HallEnv}_{\mathfrak{C}}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w})\longrightarrow H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}),\mathsf{w}),\end{gathered}

are defined as the composition of interpolation maps
[
COZZ1
, Def. 9.1]
, Hall multiplications and restrictions to the stable loci
[
COZZ1
, Def. 9.4]
. These can be defined for any quiver with potential.

Definition 2.1
.

A quiver
Q
is
symmetric
if its adjacency matrix
(\mathsf{Q}_{ij})_{i,j\in Q_{0}}
is symmetric.
A framing
\underline{\mathbf{d}}
is
symmetric
if
\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}}=\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out}}=\mathbf{d}
. In the latter case, we simplify the notations as

(2.4)

R(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})=R(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})=R(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}),\quad\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})=\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(Q,\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})=\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}).

When both
Q
and
\underline{\mathbf{d}}
are symmetric, we call
\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
a
symmetric quiver variety
.
We say that a torus action
\mathsf{A}
on the symmetric quiver variety
\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
is
self-dual
if it is induced from a self-dual action of
\mathsf{A}
on
R(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
Q
be a symmetric quiver and
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}},\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out}}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
be dimension vectors. The
symmetrization
of
R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
is defined to be
R(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{c})
(
2.4
) where
\mathbf{c}_{i}=\max\{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in},i},\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}\}
. We identify
R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
as a
G
-subrepresentation of

\displaystyle R(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{c})=R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in Q_{0}\\
\mathrm{s.t.}\,\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in},i}>\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}})\oplus\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in Q_{0}\\
\mathrm{s.t.}\,\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in},i}<\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}-\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in},i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{i}}).

Definition 2.3
.

Let
Q
be a symmetric quiver and
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}},\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out}}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
be dimension vectors and
\mathsf{A}
be a subtorus of flavour group
\operatorname{Aut}_{G}R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
. We say that the
\mathsf{A}
-action on
R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
is
pseudo-self-dual
if it extends to a self-dual
\mathsf{A}
-action on the symmetrization
R(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{c})
.
A torus
\mathsf{A}
action on
\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
is
pseudo-self-dual
if it is induced from a
pseudo-self-dual

\mathsf{A}
-action on
R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
.

Example 2.4
.

Given a quiver
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1})
, we define the
doubled quiver
to be
\overline{Q}:=(Q_{0},Q_{1}\sqcup Q_{1}^{*})
, where
Q_{1}^{*}
is the same as
Q_{1}
but with reversed arrow direction. We also define the
tripled quiver

\widetilde{Q}:=(Q_{0},Q_{1}\sqcup Q_{1}^{*}\sqcup Q_{0})
, which adds one edge loop to each node on top of
\overline{Q}
.

Fix
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
, and a cyclic stability
\theta\in\mathbb{Q}_{<0}^{Q_{0}}
, we consider two
symmetric
quiver varieties:

(1)

\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
associated with the doubled quiver
\overline{Q}
;

(2)

\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\widetilde{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
associated with the tripled quiver
\widetilde{Q}
.

The quotient map

R(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})^{s}\to\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})=R(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})^{s}/G

is a principal
G
-bundle.
Denote the associated adjoint bundle to be
\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
. Then the
moment map
for
G\curvearrowright R(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
descends to a section
\mu\in\Gamma\left(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}),\mathcal{G}^{\vee}\right)
. The total space
\mathrm{Tot}(\mathcal{G})
is naturally identified with an open subscheme
\overset{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}_{\theta}(\widetilde{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})\subseteq{\mathcal{M}}_{\theta}(\widetilde{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
consisting of
G
-equivalence classes in
R(\widetilde{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
, which are
\theta
-stable when restricted to
\overline{Q}
.
The zero locus of
\mu
:

\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}):=Z(\mu)

is the
Nakajima quiver variety

[
Nak1
,
Nak2
]
(which we assume to be nonempty).
Note that
\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
is a smooth subvariety in
\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
with codimension equals to
\mathcal{G}
, and
Z(\mu)=Z^{\mathrm{der}}(\mu)
, coincides with the derived zero locus.

Denote
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\hbar}
the torus that acts on
R(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
by assigning weights
R(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})=M\oplus\hbar^{-1}M^{\vee}
, where

\displaystyle M=\bigoplus_{a\in Q_{1}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{t(a)}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{t(a)}})\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{0}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{i}},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{v}_{i}}).

We choose torus
\mathsf{T}
which contains
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\hbar}
such that
\mathsf{T}=(\mathsf{T}/\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\hbar})\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\hbar}
, and let
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{T}/\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\hbar}
be a subtorus. Choose a function
\phi\colon\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})\to\mathbb{C}
and a section
\mu\in\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}),\mathcal{\hbar}^{-1}\mathcal{G}^{\vee})
and appropriate
\mathsf{T}
action such that
\phi
and
\mu
are
\mathsf{T}
-invariant. Let us define the
\mathsf{T}
action on edge loops by scaling with weight
\hbar
, then
\mathsf{T}
fixes
the
canonical cubic potential
:

(2.5)

\widetilde{\mathsf{w}}=\langle e,\mu\rangle=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\operatorname{tr}(\varepsilon_{i}\mu_{i}),

where
\varepsilon_{i}
is the edge loop on the node
i
, and
\mu_{i}
is the
i
-th component of moment map.

Then according to
[
COZZ1
, Ex. 6.13]
, the following

\left(\overset{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}_{\theta}(\widetilde{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}),\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}),\mu,\phi\right),\quad\left(\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}),\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}),0,\phi|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})}\right)

are compatible dimensional reduction data in the sense of
[
COZZ1
, Def. 6.2]
. In particular, for functions

\mathsf{w}:=\langle e,\mu\rangle+\phi\colon\overset{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}_{\theta}(\widetilde{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})\to\mathbb{C},\quad\mathsf{w}^{\prime}:=\phi|_{\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})}\colon\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})\to\mathbb{C},

we have (ref.
[
COZZ1
, Props. 6.5, 6.9]
):

(2.6)

\mathrm{Crit}(\mathsf{w})\cong\mathrm{Crit}(\mathsf{w}^{\prime}),\quad H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{N}_{\theta}(\overline{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}),\mathsf{w}^{\prime})\cong H^{\mathsf{T}}(\overset{\bullet}{\mathcal{M}}_{\theta}(\widetilde{Q},\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}),\mathsf{w}).

Theorem 2.5
.

(
[
COZZ1
, Thms. 9.20, 4.22, 5.5]
)

If the quiver
Q
is symmetric,
\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in},i}\geqslant\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}
for all
i\in Q_{0}
,
and
\mathsf{A}
is a pseudo-self-dual subtorus of
\mathsf{T}
, then the Hall envelopes (
2.1
)
are compatible with Hall operations (
[
COZZ1
, Def. 9.4]
). In particular, they satisfy the triangle lemma (
[
COZZ1
, Lem. 9.6]
).

When the framing is moreover symmetric, we have equalities

\mathrm{HallEnv}_{\mathfrak{C}}=\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}},

with (critical) stable envelopes
\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}
defined in
[
COZZ1
, Defs. 3.4]
.

Remark 2.6
.

We refer to
[
COZZ1
, Props. 9.23, 9.24]
when the equalities also hold but the framing is not symmetric.
This is applicable to the case when the
\mathsf{A}
-action splits the framing to
u\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}+\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime\prime}
, including all root

R
-matrices used in this paper, see for example eqn. (
4.4
).

Remark 2.7
.

The condition
\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in},i}\geqslant\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}
is necessary for the triangle lemma to hold, see
[
COZZ1
, Ex. 9.25, Prop. 9.26]
.

2.2.
R
-matrices

Definition 2.8
.

For two chambers
\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2}
in
\operatorname{Lie}(\mathsf{A})_{\mathbb{R}}
, and a generic slope
\mathsf{s}\in\mathrm{Char}(G)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
, the
R-matrices
are

\displaystyle R_{\mathfrak{C}_{2},\mathfrak{C}_{1}}

\displaystyle:=(\mathrm{HallEnv}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}})^{-1}\circ\mathrm{HallEnv}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\in\operatorname{End}\left(H^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w})_{\mathrm{loc}}\right),

Here we consider
\mathsf{T}
-localized cohomology, so inverses of Hall envelopes exist (see
[
COZZ1
, Rmk. 9.5]
).

Remark 2.9
.

The
R
matrix admits a Gauss type decomposition

(2.7)

\displaystyle R_{\mathfrak{C}_{2},\mathfrak{C}_{1}}=F_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}\cdot G_{\mathfrak{C}_{2},\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\cdot E_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}

where

\displaystyle F_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}=\mathrm{HallEnv}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}^{-1}\cdot\>e^{\mathsf{T}}(N^{-}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}),\quad G_{\mathfrak{C}_{2},\mathfrak{C}_{1}}=\frac{e^{\mathsf{T}}(N^{-}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}})}{e^{\mathsf{T}}(N^{-}_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}})},\quad E_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}=e^{\mathsf{T}}(N^{-}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}})^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{HallEnv}_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\>.

Note that
G_{\mathfrak{C}_{2},\mathfrak{C}_{1}}
is a diagonal operator,
F_{\mathfrak{C}_{2}}
and
E_{\mathfrak{C}_{1}}
are triangular with unit diagonal with respect to the partial order given by
\mathfrak{C}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{1}
respectively.

By construction, for a sequence of chambers
\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{2},\ldots,\mathfrak{C}_{n}
,
R
-matrices satisfy the following
braid relations
:

(2.8)

\displaystyle R_{\mathfrak{C}_{1},\mathfrak{C}_{n}}R_{\mathfrak{C}_{n},\mathfrak{C}_{n-1}}\cdots R_{\mathfrak{C}_{2},\mathfrak{C}_{1}}=\operatorname{id}.

Example 2.10
.

Consider a decomposition of framing vector spaces

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{d}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(i)}.

This gives an
\mathsf{A}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n}
action by
\underline{\mathbf{d}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\,\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(i)}
, where
K_{\mathsf{A}}(\operatorname{pt})=\mathbb{Q}[z_{i}^{\pm}]_{i=1}^{n}
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}):=\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}}\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}),

and denote its critical cohomology as well as
\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}
-graded components by

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}}^{\mathsf{w}}:=H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}),\mathsf{w}),\quad\mathcal{H}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}}^{\mathsf{w}}(\mathbf{v}):=H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}),\mathsf{w}),

We have an induced
\mathsf{A}
-action such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{\mathsf{A}}\cong\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(i)}).

Assume that

(2.10)

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}|_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{\mathsf{A}}}=\boxplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathsf{w}_{i},\quad\mathsf{w}_{i}\colon\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(i)})\to\mathbb{C},

then by Thom-Sebastiani isomorphism, we have

\displaystyle H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w})_{\mathrm{loc}}\cong\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{w}_{i}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(i)},\mathrm{loc}},\quad\text{and}\quad R_{\mathfrak{C}_{2},\mathfrak{C}_{1}}\in\operatorname{End}\left(\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{w}_{i}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(i)},\mathrm{loc}}\right),

where the second tensor products are taken over the base field
\operatorname{Frac}(H_{\mathsf{T}}(\operatorname{pt}))
.

Remark 2.11
.

If the derived global sections
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(i)}),\mathsf{w}_{i})
are pure for all
i
, then the Thom-Sebastiani isomorphism holds without localization (e.g.
[
Zhu
, Thm. A.1.10]
), that is,

\displaystyle H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w})\cong\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}_{1}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(1)}}\otimes_{H_{\mathsf{T}}(\operatorname{pt})}\cdots\otimes_{H_{\mathsf{T}}(\operatorname{pt})}\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{w}_{n}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(n)}}\>,

and in fact each
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{w}_{i}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(i)}}
is a free
H_{\mathsf{T}}(\operatorname{pt})
-module. For example,
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(i)}),\mathsf{w}_{i})
is pure when
\mathsf{w}_{i}=0
, or
\mathsf{w}_{i}
admits dimensional reduction to Borel-Moore homology of a variety that is pure (e.g. Nakajima quiver varieties).

2.3.
Root
R
-matrices

A general
R
-matrix is a product of
R
-matrices corresponding to pairs of chambers separated by a wall. Namely, let
\mathfrak{C},\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}
be two chambers separated by a wall
\alpha=0
. Here
\alpha
is a root and we may assume
\alpha(\mathfrak{C})>0
. Let
\mathsf{A}_{\alpha}\subset\mathsf{A}
be the subtorus with Lie algebra
\ker(\alpha)
. We denote

\displaystyle X^{\alpha}=X^{\mathsf{A}_{\alpha}}.

We note that

\displaystyle X^{\alpha}=\bigsqcup_{(\phi\colon\mathsf{A}_{\alpha}\to G)/\sim}X^{\alpha,\phi},\,\,\,\text{ where }\,X^{\alpha,\phi}=R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{\mathsf{A}_{\alpha},\phi}/\!\!/_{\theta}G^{\phi(\mathsf{A}_{\alpha})}.

For the
\mathsf{A}/\mathsf{A}_{\alpha}
-action on
X^{\alpha}
, there are two chambers, namely
\alpha\gtrless 0
. We denote the (cohomological)
root

R
-
matrix
:

\displaystyle R_{\alpha}=R_{<0,>0}\in\operatorname{End}(H^{\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{A}_{\alpha}}(X^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w})_{\mathrm{loc}}).

Proposition 2.12
.

Under the same assumptions as Theorem
2.5
, we have

(2.11)

\displaystyle R_{\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\mathfrak{C}}=R_{\alpha}.

Proof.

We have
R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{\mathsf{A}_{\alpha},\phi}=R(Q_{\phi},\mathbf{v}_{\phi},\underline{\mathbf{d}_{\phi}})
for a quiver
Q_{\phi}
with
\mathbf{v}_{\phi},\mathbf{d}_{\phi,\operatorname{in}},\mathbf{d}_{\phi,\operatorname{out}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{Q}_{\phi,0}}
determined by the homomorphism
\phi\colon\mathsf{A}_{\alpha}\to G
. The assumptions in Theorem
2.5
imply that
Q_{\phi}
is symmetric and
\mathbf{d}_{\phi,\operatorname{in},i}\geqslant\mathbf{d}_{\phi,\operatorname{out},i}
for all
i\in Q_{\phi,0}
and the induced
\mathsf{A}/\mathsf{A}_{\alpha}
action on
R(Q_{\phi},\mathbf{v}_{\phi},\underline{\mathbf{d}_{\phi}})
is pseudo-self-dual. Then it follows from the triangle lemma that

\displaystyle R_{\mathfrak{C}^{\prime},\mathfrak{C}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{HallEnv}_{\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}}^{-1}\circ\mathrm{HallEnv}_{\mathfrak{C}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{HallEnv}_{<0}^{-1}\circ\mathrm{HallEnv}_{\mathfrak{C}_{\alpha}}^{-1}\circ\mathrm{HallEnv}_{\mathfrak{C}_{\alpha}}\circ\mathrm{HallEnv}_{>0}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{HallEnv}_{<0}^{-1}\circ\mathrm{HallEnv}_{>0}

\displaystyle=R_{\alpha},

where
\mathrm{HallEnv}_{\mathfrak{C}_{\alpha}}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{\alpha},\mathsf{w})\to H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})
is the Hall envelope for the torus
\mathsf{A}_{\alpha}
in the chamber
\mathfrak{C}_{\alpha}
.
∎

Example 2.13
.

Take
n=2
in Example
2.10
, and pick chambers
\mathfrak{C}_{\pm}=\{z_{1}\gtrless z_{2}\}
, then
R_{-,+}(z_{1},z_{2})
is a root
R
-matrix corresponding to
\mathsf{A}/\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{diag}}
-action where
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{diag}}
is the diagonal torus
\{z_{1}=z_{2}\}
, in particular, it only depends on the difference of spectral parameters:

\displaystyle R_{-,+}(z_{1},z_{2})=R_{-,+}(z_{1}-z_{2}).

Assume moreover that
\mathbf{d}^{(i)}_{\operatorname{in},j}\geqslant\mathbf{d}^{(i)}_{\operatorname{out},j}
for all
j\in Q_{0}
and
i\in\{1,2\}
, then the
\mathsf{A}
action is pseudo-self-dual (see Definition
2.3
). According to
[
COZZ1
, Prop. 9.23]
, Hall envelopes agree with stable envelopes in this case (here
\mathsf{A}/\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\mathrm{diag}}
is one dimensional, the existence of stable envelopes is guaranteed by
[
COZZ1
, Prop. 3.23]
), and we have

\displaystyle R_{-,+}(z_{1}-z_{2})=(\operatorname{Stab}_{-})^{-1}\circ\operatorname{Stab}_{+}\,.

2.4.
Covers and factorizations

Assume that
\widehat{Q}\to Q
is a free abelian cover of quivers such that
\widehat{Q}
is symmetric (so
Q
is also symmetric). Let
\Gamma\cong\mathbb{Z}^{r}
be the covering group. We fix a lift of vertices

\displaystyle Q_{0}\hookrightarrow\widehat{Q}_{0},\qquad i\mapsto\widehat{i},

and set the image to be
\Gamma
-degree zero, then any other vertex in
\widehat{Q}
can be uniquely written as
\gamma\widehat{i}
for
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
i\in Q_{0}
. These data determine an embedding of groups

\displaystyle(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{r}\cong\Gamma^{\wedge}\hookrightarrow G_{\mathrm{edge}}=\prod_{i,j\in Q_{0}}\mathrm{GL}(\mathsf{Q}_{ij}),

such that
\Gamma^{\wedge}
-weight
\gamma_{a}
for an arrow
a\in Q_{1}
is determined by requiring a lift of
a:i\to j
being

(2.12)

\displaystyle\widehat{i}\to\gamma_{a}^{-1}\widehat{j}.

We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(Q,\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{\Gamma^{\wedge}}=\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\widehat{Q},\underline{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}),

where
\underline{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}
is the degree-zero lift of framing vector spaces
\underline{\mathbf{d}}
, namely

\displaystyle\underline{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}_{\gamma\widehat{i}}=\begin{cases}\underline{\mathbf{d}}_{i},&\gamma=1,\\
\underline{\mathbf{0}},&\gamma\neq 1.\end{cases}

Consider a decomposition of framing vector spaces:

\displaystyle\underline{\mathbf{d}}=u\,\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(1)}+\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(2)},

where
u
is the equivariant parameter in
K_{\mathsf{A}_{1}}(\operatorname{pt})\cong\mathbb{Q}[u^{\pm}]
for a torus
\mathsf{A}_{1}\cong\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Denote

R_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(1)},\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(2)}}(u)

the root
R
-matrix associated to the
\mathsf{A}_{1}
action. Here we regard
u
as an additive variable in
H_{\mathsf{A}_{1}}(\operatorname{pt})\cong\mathbb{Q}[u]
.

We take
\mathsf{A}_{2}=\Gamma^{\wedge}\times\mathsf{A}_{1}
. Note that
\mathsf{A}_{2}
action on
R(Q,\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
is pseudo-self-dual. Choose a generic vector
t\in\operatorname{Lie}(\Gamma^{\wedge})_{\mathbb{R}}
and represent
root

R
-
matrices in the stable basis
:

\displaystyle R_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(1)},\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(2)}}(u)_{\mathrm{stab}}:=(\mathrm{HallEnv}_{-t})^{-1}R_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(1)},\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(2)}}(u)\,\mathrm{HallEnv}_{t}\,.

Proposition 2.14
.

Assume
\mathbf{d}^{(i)}_{\operatorname{in},j}\geqslant\mathbf{d}^{(i)}_{\operatorname{out},j}
for all
j\in Q_{0}
and
i\in\{1,2\}
, then in the stable basis we have

(2.13)

\displaystyle R_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(1)},\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(2)}}(u)_{\mathrm{stab}}=\prod_{\gamma\in\Gamma}^{\longleftarrow}\widehat{R}_{\gamma\underline{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}^{(1)},\underline{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}^{(2)}}(u-\gamma),

where the product is ordered such that
\gamma(t)
is increasing from left to right.
Here
\widehat{R}_{\gamma\underline{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}^{(1)},\underline{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}^{(2)}}(u)
is the root
R
-matrix for the quiver
\widehat{Q}
with decomposition of framing

u\gamma\underline{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}^{(1)}+\underline{\widehat{\mathbf{d}}}^{(2)}
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
2.5
and
[
COZZ1
, Lem. 9.6]
.
∎

2.5.
Yang-Baxter equations

Take
n=3
in Example
2.10
, and assume moreover that
\mathbf{d}^{(i)}_{\operatorname{in},j}\geqslant\mathbf{d}^{(i)}_{\operatorname{out},j}
for all
j\in Q_{0}
and
i\in\{1,2,3\}
. Then the
\mathsf{A}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{3}
action given by
\underline{\mathbf{d}}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}z_{i}\,\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(i)}
is pseudo-self-dual (see Definition
2.3
). The roots are given by

\displaystyle\alpha_{ij}=\{z_{i}=z_{j}\},\quad i\neq j\in\{1,2,3\},

and the chambers are

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{ijk}=\{z_{i}>z_{j}>z_{k}\},\quad\{i,j,k\}=\{1,2,3\}.

Assuming (
2.10
), i.e.

\mathsf{w}|_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{\mathsf{A}}}=\boxplus_{i=1}^{3}\mathsf{w}_{i}.

The root
R
-matrix is

\displaystyle R_{ij}(z_{i}-z_{j}):=R_{\alpha_{ij}}(z_{i}-z_{j})\in\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{w}_{k}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(k)}}}\otimes\operatorname{End}\left(\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{w}_{i}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(i)}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{w}_{j}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{(j)}}\right)_{\mathrm{loc}},\qquad k\notin\{i,j\}.

The braid relation (
2.8
) implies the following
Yang-Baxter equation
.

Proposition 2.15
.

Notations as above, we have

(2.14)

\displaystyle R_{12}(z_{1}-z_{2})R_{13}(z_{1}-z_{3})R_{23}(z_{2}-z_{3})=R_{23}(z_{2}-z_{3})R_{13}(z_{1}-z_{3})R_{12}(z_{1}-z_{2}).

2.6.
Classical
R
-matrices

We start with a root
R
-matrix
R(u)=R_{-,+}(u)
(Example
2.13
) in the cohomology setting with the additional assumption that the framings are symmetric:

\mathbf{d}^{(i)}_{\operatorname{in}}=\mathbf{d}^{(i)}_{\operatorname{out}}.

Here we choose the torus such that
\mathsf{A}=\mathbb{C}^{*}_{u}
,
\mathsf{T}=\mathsf{T}_{0}\times\mathsf{A}
, so that the framing decomposes as
u\mathbf{d}^{(1)}+\mathbf{d}^{(2)}
.

For the following discussions, we also include the normalizer
\epsilon
given by (
3.34
) in the stable envelopes
[
COZZ1
, Def. 3.5]
. Furthermore, we invoke a sign twist
\Sigma
such that it acts on
H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{d}^{(1)})\times\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{d}^{(2)}),\mathsf{w}^{(1)}\boxplus\mathsf{w}^{(2)})
by the sign
(-1)^{|\mathbf{v}^{(1)}|\cdot|\mathbf{v}^{(2)}|}
, where
|\cdots|
is the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-grading (
3.2
).

Applying the Gauss decomposition (
2.7
) to
\Sigma\cdot R(u)
and we get

\Sigma\cdot R(u)=F(u)\,G(u)\,E(u).

Moreover, each factor is of form
\operatorname{id}+O(u^{-1})
, so
\Sigma\cdot R(u)
is of the following form:

(2.15)

\displaystyle\Sigma\cdot R(u)=\operatorname{id}+\frac{\boldsymbol{r}}{u}+O(u^{-2}),

where
\boldsymbol{r}
is a linear operator acting on
H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{d}^{(1)})\times\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{d}^{(2)}),\mathsf{w}^{(1)}\boxplus\mathsf{w}^{(2)})
.
We call
\boldsymbol{r}
the
classical

R
-
matrix
. It follows from Yang-Baxter equation that
\boldsymbol{r}
satisfies the
classical Yang-Baxter equations
:

(2.16)

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{r}_{12},\boldsymbol{r}_{13}+\boldsymbol{r}_{23}]=0,\qquad[\boldsymbol{r}_{23},\boldsymbol{r}_{12}+\boldsymbol{r}_{13}]=0.

Expanding
E(u)
,
G(u)
, and
F(u)
to the order
O(u^{-1})
, we find

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}=\boldsymbol{r}_{\operatorname{diag}}+\boldsymbol{r}_{\text{off-diag}}.

Here

•

the diagonal part
\boldsymbol{r}_{\operatorname{diag}}
is

(2.17)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\left(c_{1}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}^{(1)})-c_{1}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i}^{(1)})\right)\otimes\mathbf{v}^{(2)}_{i}+\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathbf{v}^{(1)}_{i}\otimes\left(c_{1}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}^{(2)})-c_{1}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i}^{(2)})\right)-\sum_{a:i\to j}t_{a}\left(\mathbf{v}^{(1)}_{i}\otimes\mathbf{v}^{(2)}_{j}+\mathbf{v}^{(1)}_{j}\otimes\mathbf{v}^{(2)}_{i}\right),

where
t_{a}
is the
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-equivariant weight of the arrow
a:i\to j
, and
c_{1}(-)
is the
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-equivariant 1st Chern class,

•

the off-diagonal part
\boldsymbol{r}_{\text{off-diag}}
is given by critical convolution using
\operatorname{can}(\boldsymbol{\rho})
, where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\rho}\in H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}}\left(X^{\mathsf{A}}\times_{X^{\mathsf{A}}_{0}}X^{\mathsf{A}}\right)\;\text{ for }\;X^{\mathsf{A}}=\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{d}^{(1)})\times\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{d}^{(2)})\;\text{ and }\;X^{\mathsf{A}}_{0}=\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathbf{d}^{(1)})\times\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathbf{d}^{(2)})

is an integral class of homological degree
\dim F+\dim F^{\prime}-2
on the connected component
F^{\prime}\times_{X^{\mathsf{A}}_{0}}F\subset X^{\mathsf{A}}\times_{X^{\mathsf{A}}_{0}}X^{\mathsf{A}}
.
\boldsymbol{\rho}
is determined by

(2.18)

\begin{gathered}(F^{\prime}\times F\hookrightarrow X\times F)^{!}[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{+}(\Delta_{F})]=\pm u^{\operatorname{rk}N^{-}_{F^{\prime}/X}-1}\boldsymbol{\rho}+\text{lower degree terms}\;,\;\text{ if $F\prec F^{\prime}$,}\\
(F^{\prime}\times F\hookrightarrow X\times F)^{!}[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{-}(\Delta_{F})]=\pm u^{\operatorname{rk}N^{+}_{F^{\prime}/X}-1}\boldsymbol{\rho}+\text{lower degree terms}\;,\;\text{ if $F^{\prime}\prec F$,}\end{gathered}

where the signs are determined by the normalizer
\epsilon
and sign twist
\Sigma
.
In particular,
\boldsymbol{\rho}
does not depend on the choice of function
\mathsf{w}^{(1)}
or
\mathsf{w}^{(2)}
.

Remark 2.16
.

In the case of a tripled quiver
\widetilde{Q}
with canonical cubic potential
\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}
(
2.5
), we have
\mathsf{T}_{0}=\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\hbar}
and the diagonal part reads

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}_{\operatorname{diag}}=\hbar\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\left(\mathbf{d}_{i}^{(1)}\otimes\mathbf{v}^{(2)}_{i}+\mathbf{v}_{i}^{(1)}\otimes\mathbf{d}^{(2)}_{i}\right)-\hbar\sum_{i,j\in Q_{0}}\mathsf{C}_{i,j}\mathbf{v}^{(1)}_{i}\otimes\mathbf{v}^{(2)}_{j},

where
\mathsf{C}_{i,j}
is the Cartan matrix for the original quiver
{Q}
. This recovers
[
MO
, (4.21)]
by dimensional reduction.

2.7.
Action of Steinberg correspondence

Let
X=\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
be a symmetric quiver variety. It is known
[
COZZ2
, Thm. 1.1]
that

•

the affinization map
X\to X_{0}=\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
is birational and small,

•

there exists a flat
\mathsf{A}
-equivariant morphism
\pi\colon X\to B
to an affine space
B
(with trivial
\mathsf{A}
-action) such that every fiber of
\pi
is an irreducible normal variety with symplectic singularities,

•

there exists an
\mathsf{A}
-invariant open dense subset
U\subset B
with
\pi|_{\pi^{-1}(U)}\colon\pi^{-1}(U)\to U
smooth and affine.

Since
X\to X_{0}
is birational and small, we have
\dim X\times_{X_{0}}X=\dim X
and the diagonal
\Delta_{X}
is the unique irreducible component of
X\times_{X_{0}}X
with dimension
\dim X
. It follows that there exists a decomposition

\displaystyle H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim X-2}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)\cong H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim X-2}(\Delta_{X})\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{C}\cdot[Z_{i}]\right),

where
\{Z_{i}\}
are irreducible components of
X\times_{X_{0}}X
of dimension
\dim X-1
.

Definition 2.17
.

A
Steinberg correspondence
is a class
\mathcal{L}\in H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim X-2}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\lambda\,[\Delta_{X}]+\sum_{i}a_{i}[Z_{i}],\;\text{ with }\;\lambda\in H_{\mathsf{T}}^{2}(\operatorname{pt}),\;a_{i}\in\mathbb{C}.

We give some criteria for a class in
H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim X-2}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
being a Steinberg correspondence.

Lemma 2.18
.

A class
\mathcal{L}\in H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim X-2}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
is a Steinberg correspondence if and only if the induced convolution on
H^{\mathsf{T}}(X)
maps the fundamental class
[X]
to
\lambda[X]
for some
\lambda\in H_{\mathsf{T}}^{2}(\operatorname{pt})
.

Proof.

The convolution induced by
[Z_{i}]
maps
[X]
to
\operatorname{pr}_{1*}([Z_{i}])
, where
\operatorname{pr}_{1}\colon X\times_{X_{0}}X\to X
is the projection to the first factor. We claim that the image of
Z_{i}
in
X
has dimension
\leqslant\dim X-2
, so
\operatorname{pr}_{1*}([Z_{i}])=0
, and the lemma then follows.
To prove the claim, we note that
\pi\colon\pi^{-1}(U)\to U
is a smooth affine morphism and the affinization map is an isomorphism when restricted to
\pi^{-1}(U)
. This implies that
Z_{i}\subseteq X\times_{X_{0}}X\times_{B}(B\setminus U)
. As
B
is affine, we have

X\times_{X_{0}}X\times_{B}\{b\}\cong\pi^{-1}(b)\times_{X_{0}}\pi^{-1}(b).

For a generic
b\in B\setminus U
,
Z_{i}\times_{B}\{b\}\subseteq\pi^{-1}(b)\times_{X_{0}}\pi^{-1}(b)
does not lie in the diagonal, as otherwise
Z_{i}
would lie in the diagonal of
X\times_{X_{0}}X
. This implies that the image of
Z_{i}\times_{B}\{b\}
in
\pi^{-1}(b)
has dimension

<\pi^{-1}(b)
. Therefore the image of
Z_{i}
in
X
has
dimension
<\dim\pi^{-1}(b)+\dim(B\setminus U)\leqslant\dim X-1
.
∎

Lemma 2.19
.

A class
\mathcal{L}\in H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim X-2}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
is a Steinberg correspondence if and only its image in
H^{\mathsf{A}}_{2\dim X-2}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
is a Steinberg correspondence.

Proof.

By assumption and Lemma
2.18
, the image of the class
\mathcal{L}([X])
in
H^{\mathsf{A}}(X)
is
\lambda[X]
for some
\lambda\in H^{2}_{\mathsf{A}}(\operatorname{pt})
. By the freeness
2
2

2

The quiver variety
X
has a
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-action scaling all arrows of the quiver, whose fixed locus is smooth and proper. By an induction argument on the Bialynicki-Birula strata, one can show the freeness by reducing to the fixed locus and using
[
Zhu
, Thm. A.1.10]
.
:

H^{\mathsf{T}}(X)\cong H(X)\otimes H_{\mathsf{T}}(\operatorname{pt}),

we conclude that
\mathcal{L}([X])=\tilde{\lambda}[X]
in
H^{\mathsf{T}}(X)
for some
\mathsf{T}
-weight
\tilde{\lambda}
.
∎

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
\mathcal{L}\in H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim X-2}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
be a class, and assume that the image of
\mathcal{L}
in
H^{\mathsf{A}}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
is in the image of the pushforward map
H^{\mathsf{A}}(X\times_{X_{0}}X\times_{B}(B\setminus U))\to H^{\mathsf{A}}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
. Then
\mathcal{L}
is a Steinberg correspondence.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.19
, it is enough to show that every class in
H^{\mathsf{A}}_{2\dim X-2}(X\times_{X_{0}}X\times_{B}(B\setminus U))
induces a Steinberg correspondence after pushing forward to
H^{\mathsf{A}}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
. The irreducible components
\{Z_{i}\}
of
X\times_{X_{0}}X
with dimension
\dim X-1
are contained in
X\times_{X_{0}}X\times_{B}(B\setminus U)
. Let codimension one (in
B
) irreducible components of
B\setminus U
be
C_{1},C_{2},\ldots
, then irreducible components of
\Delta_{X}\times_{B}(B\setminus U)\cong\pi^{-1}(B\setminus U)
with dimension
\dim X-1
are exactly
\pi^{-1}(C_{1}),\pi^{-1}(C_{2}),\ldots
, because
\pi
is flat with irreducible fibers. Since
\mathsf{A}
acts on
B
trivially, we have
H^{2}_{\mathsf{A}}(B)=0
, and it follows that
[\pi^{-1}(C_{1})]=\pi^{*}[C_{1}],[\pi^{-1}(C_{2})]=\pi^{*}[C_{2}],\ldots
are zero in
H^{\mathsf{A}}(\Delta_{X})
. Then a class in the image of the pushforward map
H^{\mathsf{A}}_{2\dim X-2}(X\times_{X_{0}}X\times_{B}(B\setminus U))\to H^{\mathsf{A}}_{2\dim X-2}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
can be written as
\sum_{i}a_{i}[Z_{i}]
with
a_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
. In particular, it is a Steinberg correspondence.
∎

The main result of this subsection is the following compatibility result between stable envelopes and Steinberg correspondences.

Theorem 2.21
.

Given a Steinberg correspondence
\mathcal{L}
,
then there exists an integral class
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{A}}\in H^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{\mathsf{A}}\times_{X_{0}}X^{\mathsf{A}})
such that for any chamber
\mathfrak{C}
, the diagram

\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,\,\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
\scriptstyle{(-1)^{\sharp}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{A}}}
\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{L}}
\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{\mathsf{A}},\mathsf{w})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,\,\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}}
\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})}

commutes after
\mathsf{A}
-localization,
where vertical arrows are convolutions induced by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{A}}
(up to sign) and by
\mathcal{L}
respectively. For a pair of connected components
F_{1},F_{2}\subset X^{\mathsf{A}}
, the sign
(-1)^{\sharp}
for
H^{\mathsf{T}}(F_{2},\mathsf{w})\to H^{\mathsf{T}}(F_{1},\mathsf{w})
is determined by

\displaystyle(-1)^{\sharp}=\frac{\prod\text{$\mathsf{A}$-weights in $N^{-}_{F_{1}/X}$}}{\sqrt{\prod\text{$\mathsf{A}$-weights in $N_{F_{1}/X}$}}}\times\frac{\prod\text{$\mathsf{A}$-weights in $N^{+}_{F_{2}/X}$}}{\sqrt{\prod\text{$\mathsf{A}$-weights in $N_{F_{2}/X}$}}}\,,

where we choose a square root and fix it for all chambers.

Proof.

By linearity, it is enough to prove for
\mathcal{L}=\lambda[\Delta_{X}]
and for
\mathcal{L}=[Z_{i}]
. In the first case, we take
\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{A}}=\lambda[\Delta_{X^{\mathsf{A}}}]
. Let us show the existence of
[Z_{i}]_{\mathsf{A}}
in the second case. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}:=[\operatorname{Stab}_{-\mathfrak{C}}]^{\mathrm{t}}\circ[Z_{i}]\circ[\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}],

and by a similar argument as
[
MO
, Thm. 4.6.1]
, the pushforward along
X\times X
in the definition of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is proper, so
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is an integral class in
H^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{\mathsf{A}}\times_{X_{0}}X^{\mathsf{A}})
. Note that for a pair of connected components
F_{1},F_{2}\subset X^{\mathsf{A}}
, the homological degree of
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
on the component
F_{1}\times_{X_{0}}F_{2}
is
\dim(F_{1}\times F_{2})-2
. By
[
COZZ1
, Lem. 3.29]
,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
makes the diagram commute for chamber
\mathfrak{C}
.
Next we will show that
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is up to a sign independent of the choice of
\mathfrak{C}
.

According to
[
COZZ2
]
, we have

(1)

F_{1}\to X_{0}
is
\mathsf{T}
-equivariant, and birational and small onto its image (ref.
[
COZZ2
, Prop. 3.21]
),

(2)

\dim F_{1}\times_{X_{0}}F_{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\dim(F_{1}\times F_{2})-1
if
F_{1}\neq F_{2}
(ref.
[
COZZ2
, Rmk. 3.24]
).

If
F_{1}\neq F_{2}
, then
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
on the component
F_{1}\times_{X_{0}}F_{2}
is a top-dimensional cycle class. If
F_{1}=F_{2}
, then
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
on the component
F_{1}\times_{X_{0}}F_{1}
is of the form (see Lemma
2.22
)

\displaystyle\mu\cup[\Delta_{F_{1}}]+\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}[H_{j}],

where
\mu\in H_{\mathsf{T}}^{2}(F_{1})
,
a_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
, and
\{H_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
are irreducible components of
F_{1}\times_{X_{0}}F_{1}
with dimension
\dim F_{1}-1
.
By the freeness
H^{\mathsf{T}}(F_{1})\cong H(F_{1})\otimes H_{\mathsf{T}}(\operatorname{pt})
(see the proof of Lemma
2.22
), there is a decomposition

\displaystyle H_{\mathsf{T}}^{2}(F_{1})\cong H^{2}(F_{1})\oplus H^{2}_{\mathsf{T}}(\operatorname{pt}).

The above decomposition is noncanonical, but the subspace
H^{2}_{\mathsf{T}}(\operatorname{pt})\subset H_{\mathsf{T}}^{2}(F_{1})
is canonical.

We claim that
\mu\in H^{2}_{\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{A}}(\operatorname{pt})
. The set-theoretic convolution is disjoint from
F_{1}\times_{X_{0}}F_{1}\times_{B}U
, so
\mu\cup[\Delta_{F_{1}}]
in the
\mathsf{A}
-equivariant cohomology can be written as
\sum_{\alpha=1}^{m}b_{\alpha}[\Delta_{F_{1}\cap\pi^{-1}(D_{\alpha})}]
, where
b_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{C}
and
\{D_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha=1}^{m}
are codimension
1
components of
B\setminus U
. Since
B
is an affine space with trivial
\mathsf{A}
action,
[D_{\alpha}]=0
in
H^{\mathsf{A}}(B)
. So
[F_{1}\cap\pi^{-1}(D_{\alpha})]=\pi^{*}([D_{\alpha}])=0
in
H^{\mathsf{A}}(F_{1})
, and this implies that
\mu\cup[\Delta_{F_{1}}]
vanishes in
H^{\mathsf{A}}(\Delta_{F_{1}})
. This proves the claim.

From the above discussions,
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is independent of
\mathsf{A}
-equivariant parameters
\mathsf{a}
, so we can compute it by sending
\mathsf{a}
to infinity in any direction. By
[
COZZ2
, Prop. 3.23]
, we have

\dim(F_{1}\times F_{2})\cap Z_{i}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\dim(F_{1}\times F_{2})-1\,.

Denote by
L_{1},L_{2},\cdots
to be the
\frac{1}{2}\dim(F_{1}\times F_{2})-1
dimensional components of
(F_{1}\times F_{2})\cap Z_{i}
, then

\displaystyle(F_{1}\times F_{2}\hookrightarrow X\times X)^{*}[Z_{i}]=\sum_{r}f_{r}[L_{r}]+\cdots\,,

where
f_{r}\in H_{\mathsf{A}}(\operatorname{pt})
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{codim}_{X\times X}(F_{1}\times F_{2})
, and dots stand for terms of smaller degree. Applying
\mathsf{A}
-localization and we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=\lim_{\mathsf{a}\to\infty}\sum_{r}\frac{f_{r}}{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}[L_{r}],

where
\varepsilon_{1}
is the product of
\mathsf{A}
-weights in
N^{-}_{F_{1}/X}
and
\varepsilon_{2}
is the product of
\mathsf{A}
-weights in
N^{+}_{F_{2}/X}
. Since
\lim_{\mathsf{a}\to\infty}\frac{f_{r}}{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}
does not depend on the direction of taking the limit
\mathsf{a}\to\infty
, we must have
f_{r}\propto\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}
. We fix square roots

\displaystyle\varsigma_{1}=\sqrt{\prod\text{$\mathsf{A}$-weights in $N_{F_{1}/X}$}},\quad\varsigma_{2}=\sqrt{\prod\text{$\mathsf{A}$-weights in $N_{F_{2}/X}$}}

and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{A}}:=\lim_{\mathsf{a}\to\infty}\sum_{r}\frac{f_{r}}{\varsigma_{1}\varsigma_{2}}[L_{r}],

Then
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}=(-1)^{\sharp}\mathcal{L}_{\mathsf{A}}
and the theorem follows.
∎

In the proof of Theorem
2.21
, we have used the following lemma.

Lemma 2.22
.

Suppose that
Y
is a symmetric quiver variety with a
\mathsf{T}
-action, and let
Y\to V
be a
\mathsf{T}
-equivariant morphism to an affine
\mathsf{T}
-variety
V
, which is birational and small onto its image. Let
\gamma
be a class in
H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim Y-2}(Y\times_{V}Y)
such that
\gamma=0
in
H^{\mathsf{A}}(Y\times_{V}Y)
, then

\displaystyle\gamma=\lambda\,[\Delta_{Y}]\;\text{ for some $\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{A}$-weight $\lambda$}\>.

Proof.

Since
Y\to V
is birational and small,
\Delta_{Y}
is an irreducible component of
Y\times_{V}Y
and other components have dimension
<\dim Y
. It follows that there exists a decomposition

\displaystyle H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim Y-2}(Y\times_{V}Y)\cong H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim Y-2}(\Delta_{Y})\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{C}\cdot[H_{i}]\right)

where
\{H_{i}\}
are irreducible components of
Y\times_{V}Y
of dimension
\dim Y-1
. Write

\gamma=c+\sum_{i}a_{i}[H_{i}],

where
c\in H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim Y-2}(\Delta_{Y})
,
a_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
. Then
\gamma=0
in
H^{\mathsf{A}}(Y\times_{V}Y)
implies that
a_{i}=0
for all
i
, and
c=0
in
H^{\mathsf{A}}_{2\dim Y-2}(\Delta_{Y})
. By the freeness
H^{\mathsf{T}}(Y)\cong H(Y)\otimes H_{\mathsf{T}}(\operatorname{pt})
, we conclude that
c=\lambda[\Delta_{Y}]
for some
\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{A}
-weight
\lambda
.
∎

3.
Drinfeld type shifted Yangian action on critical cohomology of quiver varieties

In this section, we define
(Drinfeld type) shifted Yangians from spherical nilpotent critical cohomological Hall algebras (Definition
3.6
).
Motivated by works of Nakajima
[
Nak1
]
, Neguţ
[
N
]
on Nakajima quiver varieties, we define their action on critical cohomology of quiver varieties with potentials (Theorem
3.3
, Theorem
3.7
). This generalizes various works of Varagnolo-Vasserot
[
VV1
,
VV2
]
and Yang-Zhao
[
YZ2
]
(Examples
3.9
,
3.10
).

We also describe generators of the (Drinfeld type) shifted super Yangians via matrix elements of
R
-matrices (Theorem
3.21
).

We start with the following data:

•

a quiver
Q=(Q_{0},Q_{1})
, and denote its path algebra by
\mathbb{C}Q
,

•

a torus
\mathsf{T}_{0}
that scales the arrows in
Q
,

•

a
potential
, i.e. an element
\mathsf{W}\in\mathbb{C}Q/[\mathbb{C}Q,\mathbb{C}Q]
, such that
\mathsf{W}
is
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-invariant.

For any dimension vector
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}
,

\mathsf{w}=\operatorname{tr}(\mathsf{W})

becomes a
\mathsf{T}_{0}\times G
-invariant function on the space of
\mathbf{v}
-dimensional unframed representations
R(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{0})
(
\mathbf{0}
means framing dimension is
\mathbf{0}
), where
G=\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathrm{GL}(\mathbf{v}_{i})
is the gauge group that acts on
R(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{0})
naturally. By
G
-invariance,
\mathsf{w}
descends to a
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-invariant function on the quotient stack
\mathfrak{M}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{0})=[R(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{0})/G]
, still denoted by
\mathsf{w}
.

Given a pair of framing dimension vectors
\underline{\mathbf{d}}=(\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}},\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out}})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}
, define the Crawley-Boevey quiver
Q^{\underline{\mathbf{d}}}
to be obtained from
Q
by adding one node
\infty
, and
\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in},i}
many arrows from
\infty
to
i
and
\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}
many arrows from
i
to
\infty
, for every
i\in Q_{0}
. We choose an extension of
\mathsf{T}_{0}
action from
Q
to
Q^{\underline{\mathbf{d}}}
, then choose a
framed potential

\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}\in\mathbb{C}Q^{\underline{\mathbf{d}}}/[\mathbb{C}Q^{\underline{\mathbf{d}}},\mathbb{C}Q^{\underline{\mathbf{d}}}]
such that
\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}
is
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-invariant and its restriction to
Q
is
\mathsf{W}
. The function

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}}=\operatorname{tr}(\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}})

on the space of
\underline{\mathbf{d}}
-framed representation
R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
descends to the quotient stack
\mathfrak{M}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})=[R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})/G]
as well as the stable locus
\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})=R(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{\theta\emph{-}s}/G
, and we denote the induced functions by
\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}}
. Starting from now, we renew the notation in (
2.9
) to

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}:=H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}),\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}}),\quad\mathcal{H}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}(\mathbf{v}):=H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{M}_{\theta}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}),\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}}).

For simplicity, we fix a cyclic stability
\theta\in\mathbb{Q}_{<0}^{Q_{0}}
throughout this section.

3.1.
The general case

Let
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
denote the
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-
equivariant

nilpotent critical CoHA
associated to
(Q,\mathsf{W})
, i.e.

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{N}^{Q_{0}}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}(\mathbf{v}),\quad\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}(\mathbf{v}):=H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}}(\mathfrak{M}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{0}),\mathsf{w})_{\mathfrak{M}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{0})^{\mathrm{nil}}},

decorated with cohomological Hall algebra structure of Kontsevich and Soibelman
[
KS
]
, where
\mathfrak{M}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{0})^{\mathrm{nil}}\subset\mathfrak{M}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{0})
is the moduli stack of nilpotent unframed representations of
Q
with dimension vector
\mathbf{v}
.

\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
is a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded algebra with the grading given by

(3.2)

\displaystyle|\mathbf{v}|:=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}|i|\cdot\mathbf{v}_{i},\;\,\,\text{where }|i|=1+\#(i\to i)\pmod{2}.

Throughout this section, we impose the following assumption for simplicity.

Assumption 3.1
.

The
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-weight on every edge loop of
Q
is nontrivial.

This assumption implies that
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-fixed point of
\mathrm{Rep}(Q,\delta_{i},\mathbf{0})\cong\mathbb{C}^{\mathsf{g}_{i}}
is
\{0\}
, where
\mathsf{g}_{i}
is the number of edge loops at node
i
. Note that the locus of nilpotent representations
\mathrm{Rep}(Q,\delta_{i},\mathbf{0})^{\mathrm{nil}}=\{0\}
. By
[
COZZ1
, Prop. 2.4]
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}(\delta_{i})=H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}}(\mathfrak{M}(\delta_{i},\mathbf{0}),\mathsf{w})_{\mathfrak{M}(\delta_{i},\mathbf{0})^{\mathrm{nil}}}\cong H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}}([\{0\}/\mathbb{C}^{*}]).

Let
\mathcal{L}_{i}
be the universal line bundle on
\mathfrak{M}(\delta_{i},\mathbf{0})\cong[\mathbb{C}^{\mathsf{g}_{i}}/\mathbb{C}^{*}]
, and denote a basis of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}(\delta_{i})
as
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]
-module by

e_{i,r}:=c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{i})^{r}\cap\left[\mathfrak{M}(\delta_{i},\mathbf{0})^{\mathrm{nil}}\right],\quad\forall\,r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0},

where
c_{1}(-)
denotes the
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-equivariant first Chern class.

The
spherical subalgebra

\mathcal{SH}^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
is the
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
generated by
\{\mathcal{H}^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}(\delta_{i})\}_{i\in Q_{0}}
.
Let
\mathcal{SH}^{\mathrm{nil},\mathrm{op}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
denote the
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded opposite algebra with product

f\star^{\mathrm{op}}g=(-1)^{|f|\cdot|g|}g\star f,

for homogeneous
f,g
. Denote
\left\{f_{i,r}\right\}_{i\in Q_{0}}^{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}}
to be the generators of
\mathcal{SH}^{\mathrm{nil},\mathrm{op}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
.

A generalization of
[
VV2
, Prop. 3.4]
,
[
Nak1
]
shows that

(3.3)

\displaystyle e_{i}(u):=\sum_{r\geqslant 0}e_{i,r}u^{-r-1}\mapsto\frac{(-1)^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}}}{u-c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{i})}\,[\overline{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v}+\delta_{i},\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})]

gives rise to an action of
\mathcal{SH}^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}
. Here

\overline{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v}+\delta_{i},\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})\subset{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v}+\delta_{i},\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}+\delta_{i},\underline{\mathbf{d}})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})

are certain closed subvarieties in the product, where
{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v}+\delta_{i},\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
is the locus consisting of framed quiver representations
(V^{+}=V\oplus\mathbb{C}^{\delta_{i}},D_{\operatorname{in}},D_{\operatorname{out}})
,
(V,D_{\operatorname{in}},D_{\operatorname{out}})
such that there exists a surjective map between framed quiver representations
(V^{+},D_{\operatorname{in}},D_{\operatorname{out}})\twoheadrightarrow(V,D_{\operatorname{in}},D_{\operatorname{out}})
which is identity on the framing vector spaces.
\mathcal{L}_{i}
is the line bundle on
{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v}+\delta_{i},\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
whose fiber is the kernel of
(V^{+},D_{\operatorname{in}},D_{\operatorname{out}})\twoheadrightarrow(V,D_{\operatorname{in}},D_{\operatorname{out}})
.
\overline{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v}+\delta_{i},\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
is the locus in
{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v}+\delta_{i},\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
such that the kernel of
(V^{+},D_{\operatorname{in}},D_{\operatorname{out}})\twoheadrightarrow(V,D_{\operatorname{in}},D_{\operatorname{out}})
is a nilpotent unframed
Q
-representation.

Similarly, the map

(3.4)

\displaystyle f_{i}(u):=\sum_{r\geqslant 0}f_{i,r}u^{-r-1}\mapsto\frac{(-1)^{|i|\cdot|\mathbf{v}|}}{u-c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{i})}\,[\overline{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}-\delta_{i},\underline{\mathbf{d}})]^{\mathrm{t}}

gives rise to an action of
\mathcal{SH}^{\mathrm{nil},\mathrm{op}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}
.

Define the free commutative algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{0}_{Q}:=\mathbb{C}\left[h_{i,s}\>\big|\>i\in Q_{0},s\in\mathbb{Z}\right],

and set

h_{i}(u)=\sum_{s\in\mathbb{Z}}h_{i,s}u^{-s-1}.

Definition 3.2
.

Define the
unmodified Cartan doubled Yangian

\mathcal{DSH}_{\infty}(Q,\mathsf{W})
to be the
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]
-algebra which is obtained from the free product
\mathcal{SH}^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}*\mathcal{SH}^{\mathrm{nil},\mathrm{op}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}*\mathcal{H}^{0}_{Q}
subject to the relations:

(3.5)

\displaystyle e_{i,r}f_{j,s}-(-1)^{|i|\cdot|j|}f_{j,s}e_{i,r}=\delta_{ij}\gamma_{i}h_{i,r+s},\,\,\;\text{where }\,\gamma_{i}:=\prod_{e:i\to i}t_{e},

(3.6)

\displaystyle h_{i}(z)e_{j}(w)=\zeta^{\circ}_{ij}(z)e_{j}(w)h_{i}(z),\quad h_{i}(z)f_{j}(w)=\zeta^{\circ}_{ij}(z)^{-1}f_{j}(w)h_{i}(z).

Here
\zeta^{\circ}_{ij}(z)
is the “bond factor”:

\displaystyle\zeta^{\circ}_{ij}(z)=(-1)^{\delta_{ij}+|i|\cdot|j|}\frac{\prod_{a:i\to j}(\sigma_{j}+t_{a}-z)}{\prod_{b:j\to i}(z+t_{b}-\sigma_{j})},\;\text{where $\sigma_{j}$ is the operator }e_{j,r}\mapsto e_{j,r+1},\;f_{j,r}\mapsto f_{j,r+1},\;(r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}).

It is easy to see that the multiplication map

\mathcal{SH}^{\mathrm{nil}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]}\mathcal{SH}^{\mathrm{nil},\mathrm{op}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{0}_{Q}\to\mathcal{DSH}_{\infty}(Q,\mathsf{W})

is surjective, but
the injectivity is not clear.

Let
\mathcal{H}^{0}_{Q}
act on
\mathcal{H}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}
by tautological classes:

(3.7)

\displaystyle h_{i}(z)\mapsto(-1)^{1+\mathbf{v}_{i}+|i|\cdot|\mathbf{v}|+\sum_{i\to j}\mathbf{v}_{j}}z^{\operatorname{rk}\mathsf{U}_{i}}c_{-1/z}\left(\mathsf{U}_{i}\right),\;\text{ for }\;\mathsf{U}_{i}=\sum_{a:i\to j}t_{a}\mathsf{V}_{j}-\sum_{b:j\to i}t_{b}^{-1}\mathsf{V}_{j}+\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}\>,

where
\{t_{a}\}_{a\in Q_{1}}
are
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-weights of arrows,
c_{-1/z}(-)
denotes the
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-equivariant Chern polynomial with variable
-1/z
.

Theorem 3.3
.

The assignments (
3.3
), (
3.4
), and (
3.7
) give rise to a
\mathcal{DSH}_{\infty}(Q,\mathsf{W})
action on
\mathcal{H}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}
.

Proof.

The relations (
3.6
) are easy to check. For the relation (
3.5
), we have

(3.8)

\begin{split}&\quad\,\,e_{i}(z)f_{j}(w)-(-1)^{|i|\cdot|j|}f_{j}(w)e_{i}(z)\\
&=(-1)^{\sharp}\left(\frac{[\overline{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v}+\delta_{i}-\delta_{j},\mathbf{v}-\delta_{j},\underline{\mathbf{d}})]}{z-c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{i})}\frac{[\overline{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v}-\delta_{j},\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})]}{w-c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{j})}-\frac{[\overline{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v}-\delta_{j}+\delta_{i},\mathbf{v}+\delta_{i},\underline{\mathbf{d}})]}{w-c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{j})}\frac{[\overline{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v}+\delta_{i},\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})]}{z-c_{1}(\mathcal{L}_{i})}\right).\end{split}

If
i\neq j
, then the correspondence on the RHS of(
3.8
) is trivial; if
i=j
, then the correspondence on the RHS of(
3.8
) is supported on the diagonal, and we have

\displaystyle e_{i}(z)f_{i}(w)-(-1)^{|i|}f_{i}(w)e_{i}(z)=\Delta_{*}(\beta_{i}(z,w))\in H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}}\left(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})^{\times 2},\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}}\boxminus\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}}\right)_{\Delta}.

Note that
\beta_{i}(z,w)
is completely determined by the intersection theory on
\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})
which is irrelevant to the potential. Thus we can set the potential to be zero, then let
e_{i}(z)f_{i}(w)-(-1)^{|i|}f_{i}(w)e_{i}(z)
act on
1\in H_{\mathsf{T}_{0}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}))
, and get:

\displaystyle\beta_{i}(z,w)=e_{i}(z)f_{i}(w)(1)-(-1)^{|i|}f_{i}(w)e_{i}(z)(1).

We compute the above in two steps, following similar computations on
K
-theory of Nakajima quiver varieties by Neguţ
[
N
]
.

Step 1.
Define two complexes on
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
in cohomological degrees
0
and
1
:

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{+}_{i}=\left[\mathsf{V}_{i}\xrightarrow{(B_{i},X_{a})}\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i}\oplus\bigoplus_{a:i\to j}t_{a}\mathsf{V}_{j}\right],\qquad\mathcal{C}^{-}_{i}=\left[\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}\oplus\bigoplus_{b:j\to i}t_{b}^{-1}\mathsf{V}_{j}\xrightarrow{(A_{i},X_{b})}\mathsf{V}_{i}\right].

Note that
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}^{-}_{i})=0
by the cyclic stability condition. The projection

\pi^{+}\colon\overline{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{v}-\delta_{i},\underline{\mathbf{d}})\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})

is
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-equivariantly isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}((\mathcal{C}^{+}_{i})^{\vee})
, and
\mathcal{L}_{i}
is
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-equivariantly isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}(-1)
. The projection

\pi^{-}\colon\overline{\mathfrak{P}}(\mathbf{v}+\delta_{i},\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})\to\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})

is
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-equivariantly isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}^{-}_{i})
, and
\mathcal{L}_{i}
is
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-equivariantly isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}(1)
.

Let
\mathsf{V}^{+},\mathsf{V},\mathsf{V}^{-}
denote universal bundles on
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}+\delta_{i},\underline{\mathbf{d}}),\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}),\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}-\delta_{i},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
respectively. Lemma
3.4
implies

\displaystyle e_{i,r}(1)=\pi^{+}_{*}\left(\frac{(-1)^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}}}{z-c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(-1))}\right)_{z^{-r-1}}=(-1)^{\mathbf{v}_{i}+\sum_{i\to j}\mathbf{v}^{+}_{j}}\left[z^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}-\mathbf{v}_{i}^{+}+\sum_{i\to j}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{+}}c_{-1/z}\left(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i}+\sum_{a:i\to j}t_{a}\mathsf{V}^{+}_{j}-\mathsf{V}_{i}^{+}\right)\right]_{z^{-r-1}},

\displaystyle f_{i,s}(1)=\pi^{-}_{*}\left(\frac{(-1)^{|i|\cdot|\mathbf{v}|}}{w-c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(1))}\right)_{w^{-s-1}}=(-1)^{|i|\cdot|\mathbf{v}|}\left[w^{\mathbf{v}_{i}^{-}-\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\sum_{j\to i}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{-}}c_{-1/w}\left(\mathsf{V}_{i}^{-}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\sum_{b:j\to i}t_{b}^{-1}\mathsf{V}^{-}_{j}\right)\right]_{w^{-s-1}},

where we take coefficients of
z^{-r}
and
w^{-s}
in the power series expansions respectively.

Step 2.
We have

\displaystyle f_{i,s}e_{i,r}(1)=(-1)^{\mathbf{v}^{+}_{i}+|i|\cdot|\mathbf{v}|+\sum_{i\to j}\mathbf{v}_{j}}\pi^{-}_{*}\left(\frac{z^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}-\mathbf{v}_{i}^{+}+\sum_{i\to j}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{+}}\pi^{+*}c_{-1/z}\left(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i}+\sum_{a:i\to j}t_{a}\mathsf{V}^{+}_{j}-\mathsf{V}_{i}^{+}\right)}{w-c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(1))}\right)_{z^{-r-1},w^{-s-1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\mathbf{v}^{+}_{i}+|i|\cdot|\mathbf{v}|+\sum_{i\to j}\mathbf{v}_{j}}z^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}-\mathbf{v}_{i}+\sum_{i\to j}\mathbf{v}_{j}}c_{-1/z}\left(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i}+\sum_{a:i\to j}t_{a}\mathsf{V}_{j}-\mathsf{V}_{i}\right)\pi^{-}_{*}\left(\frac{\prod_{a:i\to i}(z-t_{a}-c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(1)))}{(z-c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(1)))(w-c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(1)))}\right)_{z^{-r-1},w^{-s-1}}.

Using Lemma
3.4
again, we get:

\displaystyle f_{i,s}e_{i,r}(1)=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\mathbf{v}^{+}_{i}+|i|\cdot|\mathbf{v}|+\sum_{i\to j}\mathbf{v}_{j}}z^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}-\mathbf{v}_{i}+\sum_{i\to j}\mathbf{v}_{j}}w^{\mathbf{v}_{i}-\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\sum_{j\to i}\mathbf{v}_{j}}c_{-1/z}\left(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i}+\sum_{a:i\to j}t_{a}\mathsf{V}_{j}-\mathsf{V}_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\prod_{a:i\to i}(z-t_{a}-w)}{z-w}c_{-1/w}\left(\mathsf{V}_{i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\sum_{b:j\to i}t_{b}^{-1}\mathsf{V}_{j}\right)\Bigg|_{z^{-r-1},w^{-s-1}}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\,e_{i,r}f_{i,s}(1)=(-1)^{|i|\cdot|\mathbf{v}|+\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}}\pi^{+}_{*}\left(\frac{w^{\mathbf{v}_{i}^{-}-\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\sum_{j\to i}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{-}}\pi^{-*}c_{-1/w}\left(\mathsf{V}_{i}^{-}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\sum_{b:j\to i}t_{b}^{-1}\mathsf{V}^{-}_{j}\right)}{z-c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(-1))}\right)_{w^{-s-1},z^{-r-1}}
