e_{\alpha}^{(s)}
form a basis in the root space
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
such that for
\alpha>0

\displaystyle(e_{\beta}^{(r)},e_{\alpha}^{(s)})=\delta_{\beta,-\alpha}\>\delta_{r,s}\>.

(7)

The restriction of
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathfrak{g}}
to
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
yields a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form on
\mathfrak{g}_{0}=\mathfrak{v}\oplus\mathfrak{d}
, which is explicitly given by the matrix

(4.13)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&\operatorname{id}\\
\operatorname{id}&\boldsymbol{Q}\end{pmatrix}\quad\text{for}\quad\boldsymbol{Q}_{ij}=\sum_{a:i\to j}t_{a}+\sum_{b:j\to i}t_{b}\in\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})\>.

(8)

Let
h_{\eta}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}
be the coroot for a root
\eta
given by

\displaystyle h_{\eta}=\sum_{i}\eta_{i}\>\mathsf{d}_{i}-\sum_{i,j}\boldsymbol{Q}_{ij}\>\eta_{i}\>\mathsf{v}_{j}\>.

Then for
\alpha>0

\displaystyle[e_{\alpha}^{(s)},e_{-\alpha}^{(t)}]=(-1)^{|\alpha|}\>\delta_{s,t}\>h_{\alpha}\>.

Proof.

(1) follows from the formula (
2.17
) for the diagonal part of classical
R
-matrix. Note that the vacuum component of
\boldsymbol{r}_{\operatorname{diag}}
is
-t_{i}\cdot 1\otimes\mathbf{v}_{i}
, and
t_{i}\neq 0
for an admissible auxiliary datum
\mathfrak{c}
, so
\mathsf{v}_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. It also follows from the admissibility that
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{c}}(\delta_{i})\neq 0
(Lemma
3.15
), so we have
\mathsf{d}_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. By (
2.17
),
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
is spanned by
\{\mathsf{v}_{i}\}_{i\in Q_{0}}
and
\{\mathsf{d}_{i}\}_{i\in Q_{0}}
, and the linear independence is obvious.

\mathfrak{g}_{\eta}
is the eigenspace of adjoint action of
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
with weight
\eta
. This implies (2).

(3) and (4) follow from the same argument as
[
MO
, §5.3.7]
.

(5) and (6) follow from the same argument as
[
MO
, §5.3.8]
, and we shall not repeat here.

(7) follows by a direct computation using (6) and (
2.17
).

(8) follows from the invariance of the bilinear form
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
∎

Remark 4.24
.

The coroot
h_{\eta}
for a root
\eta
can be written as

\displaystyle h_{\eta}=\sum_{i}\eta_{i}h_{i,0}

where
h_{i,0}
is the image of the lowest mode of
h_{i}(z)=\sum_{r\geqslant 0}h_{i,r}z^{-r-1}
under the map in Theorem
4.8
.

In
[
DM
]
(see also
[
Dav2
, pp. 18]
), a Lie superalgebra
\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{BPS},\mathsf{T}_{0}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
over the base ring
\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]
is defined as the lowest component in the perverse filtration of CoHA
\mathcal{H}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
, whose Lie brackets are supercommutators induced by a sign-twisted CoHA multiplication.

In the case of a tripled quiver
\widetilde{Q}
with canonical cubic potential
\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}
(Example
2.4
), it was conjectured by the second author and proven in
[
BD
]
that

\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{BPS},\mathsf{T}_{0}}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}}\cong\mathfrak{g}_{Q}^{\mathrm{MO},+},

where
\mathfrak{g}_{Q}^{\mathrm{MO},+}
is the positive part of Maulik-Okounkov Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{Q}^{\mathrm{MO}}

[
MO
, §5.3]
. Analogously, we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}=\bigoplus_{\eta>0}\mathfrak{g}_{\eta},

and make the following conjecture.

Conjecture 4.25
.

There is a
\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})
-Lie algebra isomorphism

\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}\cong\mathfrak{g}^{\mathrm{BPS},\mathsf{T}_{0}}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})

that intertwines their actions on any state space
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}}
.

In the case of a tripled quiver
\widetilde{Q}
with canonical cubic potential
\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}
, we will show in Theorem
4.45
that the above conjecture follows from the main result of
[
BD
]
.

4.4.
Filtration and PBW basis of
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})

\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
has a
filtration
by the degree in
u
: for
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
,
F_{k}\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
is spanned by monomials
\mathsf{E}(m_{1}u^{n_{1}})\cdots\mathsf{E}(m_{r}u^{n_{r}})
with
m_{i}
constant in
u
and
\sum n_{i}\leqslant k
. The following is a generalization of
[
MO
, Thm. 5.5.1]
.

Theorem 4.26
.

\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
is generated by
\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
and multiplication by the following tautological classes

(4.14)

\displaystyle\left\{\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{V}_{i}),\>\mathrm{ch}_{k+1}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i})\right\}_{i\in Q_{0}}^{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}},

where Chern characters are
(\mathsf{T}_{0}\times\mathsf{A}^{\text{fr}})
-equivariant.
Moreover,

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}\>\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})\cong\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}[u])

with respect to the filtration by degree in
u
.

Proof.

The idea is the same as
[
MO
, Thm. 5.5.1]
. The surjectivity of the natural map
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}[u])\to\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
is deduced from the following generalization of

[
MO
, Prop. 5.5.2]
.

Claim: if
\mathsf{E}(m)=0
, then
\mathsf{E}(m\>u^{k})\in F_{k-1}\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
.

The proof of the above claim is similar to
loc. cit.
. In fact, we can assume that
m
is an eigenvector of
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
of weight
\lambda
. If
\lambda\neq 0
then for
h=\mathsf{E}(\tilde{h})\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, we have

(4.15)

\displaystyle\lambda(h)\mathsf{E}(m\>u^{k})=[\mathsf{E}(\tilde{h}),\mathsf{E}(m\>u^{k})]=[\mathsf{E}(\tilde{h}\>u^{k}),\mathsf{E}(m)]+\text{lower degree in }u

by (
4.11
). If
\lambda=0
, then
\mathsf{E}(m\>u^{k})
is a linear combination of diagonal matrix elements of the
R
-matrix. Using the Gauss decomposition
R^{\mathrm{sup}}(u)=F(u)G(u)E(u)
and the fact that
E(u)
and
F(u)
are of the form

\displaystyle\operatorname{id}+\text{ strict triangular of order }O(u^{-1}),

we have

\frac{1}{k!}\mathsf{E}(m\>u^{k})=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}(m,\mathsf{d}_{i})\>\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{V}_{i})+\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}(m,\mathsf{v}_{i})\>\mathrm{ch}_{k+1}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i})-\sum_{i,j\in Q_{0}}\boldsymbol{Q}_{ij}\>(m,\mathsf{v}_{i})\>\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{V}_{j})+\text{lower degree in }u,

where
\boldsymbol{Q}_{ij}
is the matrix in Proposition
4.23
(7), and the pairing with
\mathsf{v}_{i},\mathsf{d}_{i}\in\mathfrak{g}_{0}
is the trace pairing.
\mathsf{E}(m)=0
implies that
(m,\mathsf{v}_{i})=0
and
(m,\mathsf{d}_{i})=0
; therefore
\mathsf{E}(m\>u^{k})\in F_{k-1}\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
. This proves the claim and the surjectivity.

The injectivity is proven the same way as
[
MO
, §5.5.3]
, and we shall not repeat. Finally, (
4.15
) implies that
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
is generated by
\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
and multiplication by tautological classes (
4.14
).
∎

4.5.
Shift homomorphisms

Fix
\mu^{\prime}\leqslant\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}
. Pick
\underline{\mathbf{d}}
,
\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}
such that

\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}}=\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{\operatorname{in}},\,\,\,\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out}}-\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}}=\mu,\,\,\,\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{\operatorname{out}}-\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{\operatorname{in}}=\mu^{\prime}.

Then
\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})
is a vector bundle on
\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime})
by adding
\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out}}-\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{\operatorname{out}}
many copies of out-going framings to
\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime})
, that is
\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{0}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathsf{V}_{i},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}-\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{\operatorname{out},i}})
. Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})\to\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime})
be the bundle map. Take a framed potential
\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}}
on
\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime})
and its pullback to
\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})
is still denoted by
\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}}
.
Then we have the pullback isomorphism:

\displaystyle\pi^{*}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}}.

Consider the following auxiliary
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}
action: it acts trivially on
\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime})
and scales the fibers of the vector bundle
\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})
with weight
1
. Then

H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}\times\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}}(\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime}),\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})\cong\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[z]=:\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}}[z],

H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}\times\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}}(\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}),\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})\cong\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[z]=:\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}}[z],

and
\pi^{*}\colon\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}}[z]\to\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}}[z]
is an isomorphism whose inverse is
i^{*}
, where
i\colon\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})
is the zero section.

Let
m\in\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{c},\mathrm{loc}})[u]
, and
\mathsf{E}(m)_{z}
and
\mathsf{E}(m)^{\prime}_{z}
be the corresponding operators on
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}}[z]
and
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}}[z]
respectively. We note that
R
-matrix on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{c},\mathrm{loc}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}}[z]
is constant in
z
because
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}
acts trivially; therefore

\mathsf{E}(m)^{\prime}_{z}=\mathsf{E}(m)^{\prime}_{z=0}\>.

Lemma 4.27
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is admissible, then
i^{*}\mathsf{E}(m)_{z}\pi^{*}\in\prod\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[\mathsf{a}^{\mathrm{fr}}]}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}})[z]
is in the subalgebra
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})[z]
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove the lemma for
i^{*}\mathsf{E}^{\pm}(m)_{z}\pi^{*}
and
i^{*}\mathsf{E}^{0}(m)_{z}\pi^{*}
. Take
\mathfrak{c}=(\mathbf{a},\mathsf{T}_{0}\curvearrowright\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{a}),\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})\in\mathcal{C}
. Let

R^{\mathrm{sup}}(u)_{z}=\Sigma\cdot\operatorname{Stab}_{u<0,\epsilon}^{-1}\operatorname{Stab}_{u>0,\epsilon},\,\,\,R^{\mathrm{sup}}(u)^{\prime}_{z}=\Sigma\cdot(\operatorname{Stab}^{\prime}_{u<0,\epsilon})^{-1}\operatorname{Stab}^{\prime}_{u>0,\epsilon}

be the
R
-matrices acting on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{c},\mathrm{loc}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}}[z]
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{c},\mathrm{loc}}\otimes\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}}[z]
respectively. Note that the vector bundle
\mathcal{V}=\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{0}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathsf{V}_{i},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}-\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{\operatorname{out},i}})
is repelling in the chamber
u>0
. By
[
COZZ1
, Props. 8.2, 8.3]
, we have

\displaystyle i^{*}\operatorname{Stab}_{u<0,\epsilon}\pi^{*}=\operatorname{Stab}^{\prime}_{u<0,\epsilon}\cdot\>(-1)^{\sharp},\quad i^{*}\operatorname{Stab}_{u>0,\epsilon}\pi^{*}=e^{\mathsf{T}_{0}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{u}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}}(\mathcal{V})\cdot\operatorname{Stab}^{\prime}_{u>0,\epsilon}\cdot\>e^{\mathsf{T}_{0}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}}(\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{fix}})^{-1},

where
(-1)^{\sharp}
on the component
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{a})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}_{2},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
is
(-1)^{\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot(\mu-\mu^{\prime})}
, and
\mathcal{V}^{\mathrm{fix}}
is the
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{u}
-fixed part of the restriction of
\mathcal{V}
to
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{u}
-fixed components.

Write the Gauss decompositions
R^{\mathrm{sup}}(u)_{z}=F(u)_{z}G(u)_{z}E(u)_{z}
and
R^{\mathrm{sup}}(u)^{\prime}_{z}=F(u)^{\prime}_{z}G(u)^{\prime}_{z}E(u)^{\prime}_{z}
, as in (
4.6
) with extra
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}
equivariance. Then we have

\displaystyle i^{*}F(u)_{z}\pi^{*}=(-1)^{\sharp}\cdot F(u)^{\prime}_{z}\cdot(-1)^{\sharp},\quad i^{*}G(u)_{z}\pi^{*}=G(u)^{\prime}_{z}\cdot D(u)_{z},\quad i^{*}E(u)_{z}\pi^{*}=H_{z}\cdot E(u)^{\prime}_{z}\cdot H^{-1}_{z},

where
D(u)
and
H
are the following diagonal operators:

\displaystyle D(u)_{z}\bigg|_{\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{a})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}_{2},\underline{\mathbf{d}})}

\displaystyle=(u-z)^{\mathbf{v}_{1}\cdot(\mu-\mu^{\prime})}c_{-1/(u-z)}\left(\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{0}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathsf{V}_{1,i},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}-\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{\operatorname{out},i}})\right),

\displaystyle H_{z}\bigg|_{\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{a})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}_{2},\underline{\mathbf{d}})}

\displaystyle=z^{\mathbf{v}_{2}\cdot(\mu-\mu^{\prime})}c_{1/z}\left(\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{0}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathsf{V}_{2,i},\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out},i}-\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{\operatorname{out},i}})\right),

where
c_{(-)}(\cdots)
denotes the
(\mathsf{T}_{0}\times\mathsf{A}^{\text{fr}})
-equivariant Chern polynomial with variable
(-)
.

Obviously matrix elements of
i^{*}F(u)_{z}\pi^{*}
are contained in
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})[z]
.

D(u)_{z}
only acts on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathfrak{c}}
, so the matrix elements of
i^{*}G(u)_{z}\pi^{*}
are contained in
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})[z]
.
H_{z}
is a polynomial in
z
with coefficients given by characteristic classes of tautological bundles
\{\mathsf{V}_{j}\}_{j\in Q_{0}}
, so
H_{z}\in\mathsf{Y}^{0}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})[z]
by admissibility of
\mathcal{C}
and Lemma
4.11
. Then it follows that the matrix elements of
H_{z}\cdot E(u)^{\prime}_{z}\cdot H^{-1}_{z}
are contained in
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})(\!(z^{-1})\!)
. Since

H_{z}\cdot E(u)^{\prime}_{z}\cdot H^{-1}_{z}=i^{*}E(u)_{z}\pi^{*},

and the latter is a polynomial in
z
, the matrix elements of
i^{*}E(u)_{z}\pi^{*}
must be in
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})[z]
.
∎

Definition 4.28
.

Fix
\mu^{\prime}\leqslant\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}
.
Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is admissible. Define
parametrized shift homomorphism

\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}\colon\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\to\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})[z]

by the assignment
\mathsf{E}(m)\mapsto\left(i^{*}\mathsf{E}(m)_{z}\pi^{*}\right)
in Lemma
4.27
, and define
shift homomorphism

\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}:=\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}\big|_{z=0}\colon\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\to\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C}).

Define
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
to be the image of
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
under the projection

\displaystyle\prod\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[\mathsf{a}^{\mathrm{fr}}]}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}})\twoheadrightarrow{\prod}^{\prime}\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[\mathsf{a}^{\mathrm{fr}}]}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}}),

where the first product is taken for all state spaces with
\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out}}-\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}}=\mu
, and the second product is taken for those state spaces coming from adding out-going arrows to those with
\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{\operatorname{out}}-\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{\operatorname{in}}=\mu^{\prime}
.

Lemma 4.29
.

\mathsf{Y}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
is isomorphic to the image of
\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}\colon\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\to\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Since
M\mapsto i^{*}M\pi^{*}
induces an isomorphism
\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[\mathsf{a}^{\mathrm{fr}}]}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}})\cong\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[\mathsf{a}^{\mathrm{fr}}]}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}})
, we can rewrite the map
\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}
as the composition (restricted to
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
)

(4.16)

\prod\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[\mathsf{a}^{\mathrm{fr}}]}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}})\twoheadrightarrow{\prod}^{\prime}\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[\mathsf{a}^{\mathrm{fr}}]}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}})\cong\prod\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[\mathsf{a}^{\mathrm{fr}}]}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}})\>.

Then it follows that
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
is isomorphic to the image of
\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}
.
∎

Definition 4.30
.

Define the
wrong-way shift homomorphism

\mathsf{S}^{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}_{z}\colon\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\to\mathsf{Y}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})(\!(z^{-1})\!)

by the assignment
\mathsf{E}(m)^{\prime}\mapsto\pi^{*}\mathsf{E}(m)^{\prime}i^{*}
, where we use the identification in (
4.16
).

Remark 4.31
.

The explicit formula for
\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}
and
\mathsf{S}^{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}_{z}
on the triangular generators can be read from the proof of Lemma
4.27

(below all Chern characters/polynomials are
(\mathsf{T}_{0}\times\mathsf{A}^{\text{fr}})
-equivariant):

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}(\mathsf{E}^{-}(m))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\deg(m)\cdot(\mu-\mu^{\prime})}\mathsf{E}^{-}(m)^{\prime},

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}^{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}_{z}(\mathsf{E}^{-}(m)^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\deg(m)\cdot(\mu-\mu^{\prime})}\mathsf{E}^{-}(m)_{z},

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}(\mathsf{E}^{+}(m))

\displaystyle=z^{\deg(m)\cdot(\mu-\mu^{\prime})}\widetilde{H}_{z}\>\mathsf{E}^{+}(m)^{\prime}\>\widetilde{H}_{z}^{-1},

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}^{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}_{z}(\mathsf{E}^{+}(m)^{\prime})

\displaystyle=z^{\deg(m)\cdot(\mu^{\prime}-\mu)}\widetilde{H}_{z}^{-1}\>\mathsf{E}^{+}(m)_{z}\widetilde{H}_{z},

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}(\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{V}_{i}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{V}_{i}),

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}^{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}_{z}(\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{V}_{i}))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{V}_{i})\,,

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}(\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{diff},i}

\displaystyle))=\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{diff},i})-\frac{(\mu_{i}-\mu^{\prime}_{i})z^{k}}{k!},

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}^{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}_{z}(\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{diff},i}

\displaystyle))=\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{diff},i})+\frac{(\mu_{i}-\mu^{\prime}_{i})z^{k}}{k!}\,,

where
\mathsf{D}_{\mathsf{diff},i}:=\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i}
,
\deg(m)
is the
\mathbb{Z}^{Q_{0}}
grading of
m
,

\widetilde{H}_{z}=\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}c_{-1/z}(\mathsf{V}_{i})^{\mu_{i}-\mu^{\prime}_{i}}\>,

and
(\cdots)_{z^{n}}
means taking
z^{n}
coefficient in the power series. For example, the operators
g_{i}(u),h_{i}(u),e_{i}(u),f_{i}(u)
in the Gauss decomposition (
3.35
) are mapped under
\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}
as follows:

\displaystyle g_{i}(u)\mapsto g_{i}(u),\quad h_{i}(u)\mapsto(u-z)^{\mu_{i}-\mu^{\prime}_{i}}h_{i}(u),\quad e_{i}(u)\mapsto[(z-u)^{\mu_{i}-\mu^{\prime}_{i}}e_{i}(u)]_{u^{<0}},\quad f_{i}(u)\mapsto(-1)^{\mu_{i}-\mu^{\prime}_{i}}f_{i}(u),

where
[\cdots]_{u^{<0}}
means taking the negative power in
u
part in the power series expansion. On the other hand,
s_{z}^{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}
maps these generators as follows

\displaystyle g_{i}(u)\mapsto g_{i}(u),\quad h_{i}(u)\mapsto(u-z)^{\mu^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{i}}h_{i}(u),\quad e_{i}(u)\mapsto[(z-u)^{\mu^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{i}}e_{i}(u)]_{u^{<0}},\quad f_{i}(u)\mapsto(-1)^{\mu^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{i}}f_{i}(u).

Here we expand
(z-u)^{\mu^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{i}}e_{i}(u)
in the region
1<|u|<|z|
, and expand
(u-z)^{\mu^{\prime}_{i}-\mu_{i}}h_{i}(u)
in the region
1<|z|<|u|
.

Lemma 4.32
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be admissible, and
\mu^{\prime}\leqslant\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}
. Then
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
is generated by

\bigcup_{a\in\mathbb{C}}\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}(\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C}))\big|_{z=a}

as a
\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})
-algebra.

Proof.

From the explicit formula of
\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}
in Remark
4.31
, we see that
\mathsf{E}^{-}(m)^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{V}_{i})
, and
\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i})
is contained in the image of
\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}\big|_{z=0}
. Moreover,
\mathsf{E}^{+}(m)^{\prime}
appears as the leading coefficient of
\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}(\mathsf{E}^{+}(m))
, so it can be generated from
\bigcup_{a\in\mathbb{C}}\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}(\mathsf{E}^{+}(m))\big|_{z=a}
. Since
\mathsf{E}^{\pm}(m)^{\prime}
,
\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{V}_{i})
, and
\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i})
generate
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
by Proposition
4.12
, the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 4.33
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be admissible, and
\mu^{\prime}\leqslant\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}
. Then the wrong-way shift homomorphism
\mathsf{S}^{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}_{z}
is injective.

Proof.

This follows from the fact that

\displaystyle{\prod}^{\prime}\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[\mathsf{a}^{\mathrm{fr}}]}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}})(\!(z^{-1})\!)\longrightarrow\prod\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[\mathsf{a}^{\mathrm{fr}}]}(\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{d}}^{\prime},\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}},\mathrm{loc}})(\!(z^{-1})\!),\qquad X\mapsto\pi^{*}Xi^{*}

is an isomorphism.
∎

The following is obvious from definition:

Lemma 4.34
.

If
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
, then the subalgebra
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\subseteq\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
is closed under parameterized shift and wrong-way shift homomorphisms, that is, the following diagrams commute

\textstyle{\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})[z]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime};z}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})[z]}

\textstyle{\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{S}^{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}_{z}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Y}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})(\!(z^{-1})\!)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{S}^{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}_{z}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Y}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})(\!(z^{-1})\!)}

Proposition 4.35
.

Shift homomorphisms are associative, that is,

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{\mu^{\prime},\mu^{\prime\prime}}\circ\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}=\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime\prime}},\quad\mu^{\prime\prime}\leqslant\mu^{\prime}\leqslant\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}\>.

Shift homomorphisms are compatible with coproducts in the following sense

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mu^{\prime}_{1},\mu_{2}}\circ\mathsf{S}_{\mu_{1}+\mu_{2},\mu^{\prime}_{1}+\mu_{2}}=(\mathsf{S}_{\mu_{1},\mu^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes\tau^{\mu_{1}-\mu^{\prime}_{1}})\circ\Delta_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}\>.

Here
\tau^{\nu}

(\nu\in\mathbb{Z}^{Q_{0}})
is the automorphism

(4.17)

\tau^{\nu}\colon\mathsf{Y}_{\mu_{2}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\to\mathsf{Y}_{\mu_{2}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C}),

given by
x\mapsto(-1)^{\deg(x)\cdot\nu}x
on homogeneous element
x
, where
\deg(x)
is the
\mathbb{Z}^{Q_{0}}
grading on
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu_{2}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

The associativity of shift homomorphisms follows from associativity of pullback isomorphisms. Let
\underline{\mathbf{d}_{1}},\underline{\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1}},\underline{\mathbf{d}_{2}}
be framing dimension vectors such that the differences between out-going and in-coming dimensions are
\mu_{1},\mu^{\prime}_{1},\mu_{2}
respectively. Consider the vector bundle projections constructed in the beginning of this subsection:

\pi\colon\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}_{1}})\to\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1}}),\quad\widetilde{\pi}\colon\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}_{1}}+\underline{\mathbf{d}_{2}})\to\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1}}+\underline{\mathbf{d}_{2}}).

The vector bundle corresponding to
\widetilde{\pi}
is attracting in the chamber
a>0
under the splitting
a\underline{\mathbf{d}_{1}}+\underline{\mathbf{d}_{2}}
, then according to
[
COZZ1
, Prop. 8.2]
, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Stab}_{+,\epsilon}\circ\>(\pi^{*}\otimes\operatorname{id})=\widetilde{\pi}^{*}\circ\operatorname{Stab}^{\prime}_{+,\epsilon}\cdot\>(-1)^{\flat},

where
(-1)^{\flat}
on the component
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}_{1},\underline{\mathbf{d}^{\prime}_{1}})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}_{2},\underline{\mathbf{d}_{2}})
is
(-1)^{\mathbf{v}_{2}\cdot(\mu_{1}-\mu^{\prime}_{1})}
. Then the compatibility with coproduct follows from the above equation.
∎

Proposition 4.36
.

The shift homomorphism
\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}
is injective. As a result,
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
.

The proof strategy is to first show that
\mathsf{S}_{0,\mu}
is injective (Lemma
4.38
), based on the technical Lemma
4.37
, then to deduce the injectivity for general
\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}
using a formal argument.

Lemma 4.37
.

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is admissible. Let
\mathcal{H}_{0}
be a state space with zero shift, and let
\left\{\mathcal{H}_{i}:=\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}_{i}}_{\underline{\delta_{i}}}\right\}_{i\in Q_{0}}
be a collection of admissible auxiliary spaces (regarded as state spaces).
Then the natural map

\displaystyle\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\prod_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{n}}\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[a_{0},a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}}]}\left(\mathcal{H}_{0}[a_{0}]\otimes\mathcal{H}_{i_{1}}[a_{i_{1}}]\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{H}_{i_{n}}[a_{i_{n}}]\right)

is injective. Here
a_{0},a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}}
are equivariant parameters of torus
\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{n+1}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{i_{j}}[a_{i_{j}}]
stands for
\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}
acting on
\mathcal{H}_{i_{j}}
with weight
a_{i_{j}}
.

Proof.

Let
\overline{\mathsf{Y}}
be the image of
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
in

\prod\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[a_{0},a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}}]}\left(\mathcal{H}_{0}[a_{0}]\otimes\mathcal{H}_{i_{1}}[a_{i_{1}}]\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{H}_{i_{n}}[a_{i_{n}}]\right).

Using Theorem
4.26
, it is enough to show that
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}[u])\to\mathrm{gr}\>\overline{\mathsf{Y}}
is injective. According to
[
MO
, §5.5.3]
, it boils down to showing that the linear map
\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}\to\overline{\mathsf{Y}}
is injective. Since
\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}\to\overline{\mathsf{Y}}
is
\mathbb{Z}^{Q_{0}}
-graded, we need to show injectivity for every homogeneous subspace
\mathfrak{g}_{\eta}
. Assume that
\mathsf{x}=\sum a_{i}\mathsf{v}_{i}+\sum b_{i}\mathsf{d}_{i}
is in the kernel. Then
\mathsf{x}
acts on
\mathcal{H}_{i}(\mathbf{0})
trivially, so
b_{i}=0
by the fact that the value of
\mathsf{d}_{i}
on
\mathcal{H}_{j}
is nonzero multiple of
\delta_{ij}
.
\mathsf{x}
acts on
\mathcal{H}_{i}(\delta_{i})
trivially, so
a_{i}=0
by the fact that the value of
\mathsf{v}_{i}
on
\mathcal{H}_{j}(\delta_{j})
is
\delta_{ij}
. This shows injectivity for
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. For
\eta
with
\eta_{i}>0
, the same argument as
[
MO
, Prop. 5.3.4]
shows that the
\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
-action maps

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{\eta}\to\mathcal{H}_{i}(\eta),\quad\mathfrak{g}_{-\eta}\to\mathcal{H}_{i}(\eta)^{\vee}

that take
\xi\in\mathfrak{g}_{\eta}
to
\xi\>|\mathbf{0}\rangle
and dually for
\mathfrak{g}_{-\eta}
, are injective. This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.38
.

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is admissible, then the shift homomorphism
\mathsf{S}_{0,\mu}\colon\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\to\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
is injective. As a result, the image
\mathsf{Y}_{0,\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
of
\mathsf{S}_{0,\mu}
is isomorphic to
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.35
, we have

\Delta_{\mu,0}\circ\mathsf{S}_{0,\mu}=(\mathsf{S}_{0,\mu}\otimes\tau^{-\mu})\circ\Delta,

and it is enough to show that
(\mathsf{S}_{0,\mu}\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta
is injective since
\tau^{-\mu}
is an automorphism. The image of
(\mathsf{S}_{0,\mu}\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta
is contained in the subalgebra
\mathsf{Y}_{0,\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\widehat{\otimes}\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
. Applying Lemma
4.37
to
\mathcal{H}_{0}
coming from adding
-\mu
framing arrows to a state space with shift
\mu
, and
\{\mathcal{H}_{i}\}_{i\in Q_{0}}

a collection of admissible auxiliary spaces (regarded as state spaces), we see that the induced map

\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\to\mathsf{Y}_{0,\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\widehat{\otimes}\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})

is injective. This implies the injectivity of
(\mathsf{S}_{0,\mu}\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
4.36
.

By Proposition
4.35
, there is commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{0,\mu}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\widehat{\otimes}\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{S}_{0,\mu^{\prime}-\mu}\otimes\tau^{\mu-\mu^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta_{\mu^{\prime}-\mu,\mu}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Y}_{\mu^{\prime}-\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\widehat{\otimes}\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C}).}

The right vertical arrow is injective since
\tau^{\mu-\mu^{\prime}}
(
4.17
) is an automorphism and
\mathsf{S}_{0,\mu^{\prime}-\mu}
is injective (Lemma
4.38
). The top horizontal arrow is injective because
(\varepsilon\otimes\operatorname{id})\circ\Delta_{0,\mu}=\operatorname{id}
(Remark
4.15
). Thus
(\mathsf{S}_{0,\mu^{\prime}-\mu}\otimes\tau^{\mu-\mu^{\prime}})\circ\Delta_{0,\mu}
is injective, and it follows from the
commutativity of the above diagram
that
\mathsf{S}_{\mu,\mu^{\prime}}
is injective.
∎

We deduce the following generalization of Lemma
4.37
,
which will be used in §
6.3
.

Proposition 4.39
.

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is admissible. Let
\mathcal{H}_{0}
be a state space with shift
\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out}}-\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}}=\mu
, and let
\left\{\mathcal{H}_{i}:=\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}_{i}}_{\underline{\delta_{i}}}\right\}_{i\in Q_{0}}
be a collection of admissible auxiliary spaces (regarded as state spaces). Then the natural map

\displaystyle\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\prod_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{n}}\operatorname{End}^{\mathbb{Z}/2}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[a_{0},a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}}]}\left(\mathcal{H}_{0}[a_{0}]\otimes\mathcal{H}_{i_{1}}[a_{i_{1}}]\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{H}_{i_{n}}[a_{i_{n}}]\right)

is injective.

Proof.

This follows from Lemmata
4.33
and
4.37
.
∎

4.6.
Explicit formulas for coproducts

In this subsection, we write down explicit formulas for some elements under the coproduct
\Delta\colon\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})\to\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})\widehat{\otimes}\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
.

By the construction of the coproduct in (
4.9
), we immediately see that

(4.18)

\displaystyle\Delta(X_{i,0})=X_{i,0}\otimes 1+1\otimes X_{i,0},\;\text{ for }\;X=e,f,h\,.

Here
X_{i,r}
for
X=e,f,h
are images of the corresponding generators of
\mathcal{D}\widetilde{\mathcal{SH}}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
under the map in Theorem
4.8
.

The coproduct of the Chern characters of the framing vector spaces is also primitive:

(4.19)

\displaystyle\Delta(\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i}))=\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i})\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i}).

The coproduct of the Chern characters of the tautological bundles are complicated, and we have the following explicit formula for the coproduct of the first Chern class.

Lemma 4.40
.

For
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}^{Q_{0}}
, let
c_{1}(\lambda)=\sum\lambda_{i}\>c_{1}(\mathsf{V}_{i})
, then

(4.20)

\displaystyle\Delta c_{1}(\lambda)=c_{1}(\lambda)\otimes 1+1\otimes c_{1}(\lambda)-\sum_{\alpha>0}\alpha(\lambda)\sum_{s}(-1)^{|\alpha|}\>e_{-\alpha}^{(s)}\otimes e_{\alpha}^{(s)},

where the sum is taken for all positive roots, and
\left\{e_{\alpha}^{(s)}\right\}
is the basis of
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}
in Proposition
4.23
(6).

Proof.

First, the Gauss decomposition (
3.35
) yields

\displaystyle c_{1}(\delta_{i})=\frac{-1}{t_{i}}\mathsf{E}(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|\>u)+t_{i}\frac{\mathsf{v}_{i}(\mathsf{v}_{i}+1)}{2}\>,

where
\delta_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{Q_{0}}
is the unit vector for the
i
-th coordinate,
|\mathbf{0}\rangle
is the vector defined in Notation
3.16
for an admissible auxiliary datum
(\delta_{i},\mathsf{T}_{0}\curvearrowright\mathcal{M}(\delta_{i}),\mathsf{w}_{i})
. Then (
4.20
) for
\lambda=\delta_{i}
follows from the same computation as
[
MO
, §10.1.2]
, namely,

\displaystyle\Delta\>\mathsf{E}(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|\>u)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|\>u)\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{E}(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|\>u)+\sum_{j\in Q_{0}}\langle\mathbf{0}|\mathsf{d}_{j}^{2}|\mathbf{0}\rangle\>\mathsf{v}_{j}\otimes\mathsf{v}_{j}+\sum_{\alpha,\beta>0}\sum_{s,t}(-1)^{|\beta|}\langle\mathbf{0}|e^{(s)}_{-\beta}\>e^{(t)}_{\alpha}|\mathbf{0}\rangle\>e^{(t)}_{-\alpha}\otimes e^{(s)}_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{E}(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|\>u)\otimes 1+1\otimes\mathsf{E}(|\mathbf{0}\rangle\langle\mathbf{0}|\>u)+t_{i}^{2}\mathsf{v}_{i}\otimes\mathsf{v}_{i}+t_{i}\sum_{\alpha>0}\alpha_{i}\sum_{s}(-1)^{|\alpha|}e_{-\alpha}^{(s)}\otimes e_{\alpha}^{(s)}\>,

and

\displaystyle\Delta\>\frac{\mathsf{v}_{i}(\mathsf{v}_{i}+1)}{2}=\frac{\mathsf{v}_{i}(\mathsf{v}_{i}+1)}{2}\otimes 1+1\otimes\frac{\mathsf{v}_{i}(\mathsf{v}_{i}+1)}{2}+\mathsf{v}_{i}\otimes\mathsf{v}_{i}\>.

For a general
\lambda
, (
4.20
) follows by linearity.
∎

If we set

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}_{+}=\sum_{\alpha>0}\sum_{s}(-1)^{|\alpha|}\>e_{-\alpha}^{(s)}\otimes e_{\alpha}^{(s)}\,,

then (
4.20
) can be written as

\displaystyle\Delta c_{1}(\lambda)=c_{1}(\lambda)\otimes 1+1\otimes c_{1}(\lambda)+\left[\sum\lambda_{i}\mathsf{v}_{i}\otimes 1\,,\,\boldsymbol{r}_{+}\right].

Using the commutation relations

[c_{1}(\mathsf{V}_{i}),e_{i,r}]=e_{i,r+1},\quad[c_{1}(\mathsf{V}_{i}),f_{i,r}]=-f_{i,r+1},

and (
3.19
), the formulas (
4.18
) and (
4.20
) determine
\Delta(X_{i,r})
for
X=e,f,h
and all
r>0
. We compute
\Delta(X_{i,1})
explicitly as follows.

Proposition 4.41
.

The coproducts of
X_{i,1}
for
X=e,f,h
are given by

(4.21)

\begin{split}\Delta(e_{i,1})&=e_{i,1}\otimes 1+1\otimes e_{i,1}+h_{i,0}\otimes e_{i,0}-\left[1\otimes e_{i,0}\,,\,\boldsymbol{r}_{+}\right],\\
\Delta(f_{i,1})&=f_{i,1}\otimes 1+1\otimes f_{i,1}+f_{i,0}\otimes h_{i,0}+[f_{i,0}\otimes 1\,,\,\boldsymbol{r}_{+}],\\
\Delta(h_{i,1})&=h_{i,1}\otimes 1+1\otimes h_{i,1}+h_{i,0}\otimes h_{i,0}+[h_{i,0}\otimes 1\,,\,\boldsymbol{r}_{+}]\\
&=h_{i,1}\otimes 1+1\otimes h_{i,1}+h_{i,0}\otimes h_{i,0}+\sum_{\alpha>0}(\delta_{i},\alpha)_{\boldsymbol{Q}}\sum_{s}(-1)^{|\alpha|}\>e_{-\alpha}^{(s)}\otimes e_{\alpha}^{(s)},\end{split}

where
\delta_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{Q_{0}}
is the unit vector for the
i
-th coordinate,
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\boldsymbol{Q}}
is the bilinear form induced by the matrix
\boldsymbol{Q}
in (
4.13
), that is,
(a,b)=\sum a_{k}\,\boldsymbol{Q}_{kl}\,b_{l}
.

Proof.

Since
\Delta
is an algebra homomorphism, we have

\displaystyle\Delta(e_{i,1})=[\Delta(c_{1}(\mathsf{V}_{i})),\Delta(e_{i,0})]=\left[\square(c_{1}(\mathsf{V}_{i}))+\left[\mathsf{v}_{i}\otimes 1\,,\,\boldsymbol{r}_{+}\right]\,,\,\square(e_{i,0})\right]\,,

where
\square(x)=x\otimes 1+1\otimes x
. It is easy to see that
[\square(c_{1}(\mathsf{V}_{i})),\square(e_{i,0})]=\square(e_{i,1})
and
[\mathsf{v}_{i}\otimes 1,\square(e_{i,0})]=e_{i,0}\otimes 1
.

We claim that

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{r}_{+},\square(e_{i,0})]=-h_{i,0}\otimes e_{i,0}\,,\quad[\boldsymbol{r}_{+},\square(f_{i,0})]=f_{i,0}\otimes h_{i,0}\,.

In fact, the classical Yang-Baxter equation (
2.16
) implies that
[\boldsymbol{r},\square(x)]=0
for any
x\in\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}
. Write
\boldsymbol{r}=\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{diag}}+\boldsymbol{r}_{+}+\boldsymbol{r}_{-}
, then we have

\displaystyle[\boldsymbol{r}_{+},\square(e_{i,0})]\in\mathfrak{g}_{\leqslant 0}\otimes\mathfrak{g}_{>0}\,,\quad[\boldsymbol{r}_{\mathrm{diag}},\square(e_{i,0})]=h_{i,0}\otimes e_{i,0}+e_{i,0}\otimes h_{i,0}\,,\quad[\boldsymbol{r}_{-},\square(e_{i,0})]\in\mathfrak{g}_{>0}\otimes\mathfrak{g}_{\leqslant 0}\,,

where
\mathfrak{g}_{\gtrless 0}=\bigoplus_{\eta\gtrless 0}\mathfrak{g}_{\eta}
,
\mathfrak{g}_{\geqslant 0}=\mathfrak{g}_{>0}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{0}
, and
\mathfrak{g}_{\leqslant 0}=\mathfrak{g}_{<0}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{0}
. Comparing degrees, we get
[\boldsymbol{r}_{+},\square(e_{i,0})]=-h_{i,0}\otimes e_{i,0}
. A similar argument shows
[\boldsymbol{r}_{+},\square(f_{i,0})]=f_{i,0}\otimes h_{i,0}
. This proves the claim.

Then

\displaystyle\Delta(e_{i,1})

\displaystyle=\square(e_{i,1})-\left[\mathsf{v}_{i}\otimes 1\,,\,h_{i,0}\otimes e_{i,0}\right]+\left[e_{i,0}\otimes 1\,,\,\boldsymbol{r}_{+}\right]=\square(e_{i,1})+\left[e_{i,0}\otimes 1\,,\,\boldsymbol{r}_{+}\right]

\displaystyle=\square(e_{i,1})+h_{i,0}\otimes e_{i,0}-\left[1\otimes e_{i,0}\,,\,\boldsymbol{r}_{+}\right],

and

\displaystyle\Delta(f_{i,1})

\displaystyle=\left[\square(f_{i,0})\,,\,\square(c_{1}(\mathsf{V}_{i}))+\left[\mathsf{v}_{i}\otimes 1\,,\,\boldsymbol{r}_{+}\right]\right]=\square(f_{i,1})+\left[f_{i,0}\otimes 1\,,\,\boldsymbol{r}_{+}\right]-\left[\mathsf{v}_{i}\otimes 1\,,\,f_{i,0}\otimes h_{i,0}\right]\,

\displaystyle=\square(f_{i,1})+\left[f_{i,0}\otimes 1\,,\,\boldsymbol{r}_{+}\right]+f_{i,0}\otimes h_{i,0}\,.

Using the relation
[e_{i,0},f_{i,1}]=(-1)^{(\delta_{i}|\delta_{i})}\gamma_{i}h_{i,1}
, we get

\displaystyle\Delta(h_{i,1})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(\delta_{i}|\delta_{i})}\gamma_{i}^{-1}[\Delta(e_{i,0}),\Delta(f_{i,1})]

\displaystyle=\square(h_{i,1})+h_{i,0}\otimes h_{i,0}+[h_{i,0}\otimes 1\,,\,\boldsymbol{r}_{+}]+(-1)^{(\delta_{i}|\delta_{i})+|i|}\gamma_{i}^{-1}\left([f_{i,0}\otimes 1\,,\,[\square(e_{i,0}),\boldsymbol{r}_{+}]]+f_{i,0}\otimes[e_{i,0},h_{i,0}]\right).

The term inside the last bracket vanishes since
[f_{i,0}\otimes 1\,,\,[\square(e_{i,0}),\boldsymbol{r}_{+}]]=[f_{i,0}\otimes 1\,,\,h_{i,0}\otimes e_{i,0}]=-\boldsymbol{Q}_{ii}f_{i,0}\otimes e_{i,0}
and
f_{i,0}\otimes[e_{i,0},h_{i,0}]=\boldsymbol{Q}_{ii}f_{i,0}\otimes e_{i,0}
. Finally, direct computation shows that

[h_{i,0}\otimes 1\,,\,\boldsymbol{r}_{+}]=\sum_{\alpha>0}(\delta_{i},\alpha)_{\boldsymbol{Q}}\sum_{s}(-1)^{|\alpha|}\>e_{-\alpha}^{(s)}\otimes e_{\alpha}^{(s)}.\qed

Remark 4.42
.

The coproduct formulas (
4.18
) and (
4.21
) resemble those coproduct formulas in
[
GNW
, Def. 4.6]
for a symmetrizable Kac-Moody Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{A,D}
associated to a generalized Cartan matrix
A
with symmetrizer
D
(see Appendix §
A.1
for definition). We expect that the composition of algebra homomorphisms

(4.22)

\varrho\colon Y_{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{A,D})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hbar]}\mathbb{C}(\hbar)\xrightarrow{\text{Ex.\ref{ex on gen symi}}}\mathcal{D}\widetilde{\mathcal{SH}}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hbar]}\mathbb{C}(\hbar)\xrightarrow{\text{Thm.\ref{thm drinfeld yangian map to rtt yangian}}}\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W})

is a bialgebra homomorphism, where
(\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W})
is the quiver with potential in Appendix §
A.1
.

We note that the induced map
\bar{\varrho}\colon\mathfrak{g}_{A,D}\to\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}
is always injective
4
4

4

This can be proven as follows. The Cartan subalgebra of
\mathfrak{g}_{A,D}
is spanned by
\{\psi_{i,0}\}_{i\in\widetilde{Q}_{0}}
in Example
3.9
, and
\bar{\varrho}(\psi_{i,0})=h_{i,0}
, so
\bar{\varrho}
is injective on the Cartan subalgebra. Since
\bar{\varrho}
is
\mathbb{Z}^{\widetilde{Q}_{0}}
-graded, so is
\ker(\bar{\varrho})
. Suppose
x\in\ker(\bar{\varrho})
is a nontrivial element in the
\alpha
root space, then by
[
Kac
, Thm. 2.2]
there exists
y
in
-\alpha
root space of
\mathfrak{g}_{A,D}
such that
[x,y]\neq 0
. Since
[x,y]
is in the Cartan subalgebra and
\bar{\varrho}([x,y])=0
,
[x,y]=0
, a contradiction.
. By comparing (
4.18
) (
4.21
) with
[
GNW
, (4.7)]
,
\varrho
is a bialgebra homomorphism if and only if any root space
\mathfrak{g}_{\eta}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}
with
(\delta_{i},\eta)_{\boldsymbol{Q}}\neq 0
for some
i\in\widetilde{Q}_{0}
is contained in the image of
\bar{\varrho}
. This is the case for the tripled affine type
A^{(1)}_{n-1}
(
n>2
) quiver with canonical cubic potential
[
Jin
]
, so the natural map

(4.23)

\varrho\colon Y_{0}(\widehat{\mathfrak{sl}}_{n})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hbar,\varepsilon]}\mathbb{C}(\hbar,\varepsilon)\xrightarrow{\text{Ex.\ref{ex on vv ex}}}\mathcal{D}\widetilde{\mathcal{SH}}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hbar,\varepsilon]}\mathbb{C}(\hbar,\varepsilon)\xrightarrow{\text{Thm.\ref{thm drinfeld yangian map to rtt yangian}}}\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})

is a bialgebra homomorphism, where
(Q,\mathsf{W})
is the quiver with potential associated to the parity sequence
(s_{i}=1:1\leqslant i\leqslant n)
in Appendix §
A.2
.

Besides the affine type
A^{(1)}_{n-1}
(
n>2
) cases, we also have the compatibility of coproducts in the finite type cases.

Theorem 4.43
.

When
A
is of finite type and indecomposable, i.e.
\mathfrak{g}_{A,D}
is a finite dimensional simple Lie algebra, then the map
(
4.22
):

\varrho\colon Y_{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{A,D})_{\mathrm{loc}}\to\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W})

is an injective bialgebra homomorphism, where
Y_{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{A,D})_{\mathrm{loc}}=Y_{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{A,D})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\hbar]}\mathbb{C}(\hbar)
. Moreover, there is a bialgebra isomorphism

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varrho}\colon Y_{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{A,D})_{\mathrm{loc}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{d}_{i,k}:i\in\widetilde{Q}_{0},\,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}]\cong\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}).

Here
\mathsf{d}_{i,k}
are primitive for all
i
and
k
,
\widetilde{\varrho}
on the first component is
\varrho
, and
\widetilde{\varrho}(\mathsf{d}_{i,k})
is the multiplication by
\mathrm{ch}_{k+1}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i})
operator when acting on a state space with framing vector spaces
\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in}},\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out}}
.

Proof.

We claim that
\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}
is finite dimensional. Consider the dimension vector
\delta_{i}
and symmetric quiver variety
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\delta_{i})
with framed potential
\mathsf{W}_{i}=\mathsf{W}+B\Phi_{i}A
where
A
is the in-coming framing,
\Phi_{i}
is the edge loop of node
i
, and
B
is the out-going framing. According to the proof of
[
VV3
, Cor. 5.2]
and
[
VV3
, Rmk. 5.3]
, there are only finitely many
\mathbf{v}
such that
\mathrm{Crit}_{\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\delta_{i})}(\operatorname{tr}\mathsf{W}_{i})
is nonempty. In particular,
\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}_{i}}_{\underline{\delta_{i}},\mathrm{loc}}=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{v}}H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\delta_{i}),\operatorname{tr}\mathsf{W}_{i})_{\mathrm{loc}}
is a finite dimensional
\mathbb{C}(\hbar)
vector space. The proof of Lemma
4.37
implies that the
\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}
-action map

\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}^{+}\to\bigoplus_{i\in\widetilde{Q}_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{\mathsf{W}_{i}}_{\underline{\delta_{i}},\mathrm{loc}}\,,\quad\xi\in\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}^{+}\mapsto(\xi\,|\mathbf{0}\rangle_{i})_{i\in\widetilde{Q}_{0}}

is injective. This implies that
\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}^{+}
is finite dimensional. So
\dim\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}=2\dim\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}^{+}+2|Q_{0}|<\infty
.

Since
\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}
is a finite dimensional
\mathbb{C}(\hbar)
Lie algebra with a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form, it is reductive, together with a triangular decomposition
\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}=\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}^{+}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{0}\oplus\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}^{-}
. The root grading on
\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}
is the same as the
\widetilde{Q}_{0}
grading. It is well-known that the nilradical of the Borel subalgebra of a reductive Lie algebra is generated by the simple root vectors, so
\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}^{+}
is generated by
\{e_{i,0}\}_{i\in\widetilde{Q}_{0}}
. This implies that the induced map
\bar{\varrho}\colon\mathfrak{g}_{A,D}\to\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}
maps
\mathfrak{g}_{A,D}^{\pm}
surjectively onto
\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}^{\pm}
, and by Remark
4.42
,
\bar{\varrho}
induces isomorphisms
\mathfrak{g}_{A,D}^{\pm}\cong\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}^{\pm}
and
\varrho
is a bialgebra homomorphism.

Consider a bialgebra
\mathfrak{D}:=\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{d}_{i,k}:i\in\widetilde{Q}_{0},\,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}]
which we require that
\mathsf{d}_{i,k}
are primitive for all
i
and
k
.
\mathfrak{D}
is a sub-bialgebra of
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W})
via the identification

\mathsf{d}_{i,k}\mapsto\mathrm{multiplication}\,\,\mathrm{by}\,\,\mathrm{ch}_{k+1}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i})\,\,\mathrm{operator}.

Then the map

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varrho}\colon Y_{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{A,D})_{\mathrm{loc}}\otimes\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{d}_{i,k}:i\in\widetilde{Q}_{0},\,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}]\to\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W})

induced by
\varrho
and the inclusion
\mathfrak{D}\subset\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W})
is a bialgebra homomorphism. The source of
\widetilde{\varrho}
is filtered by setting
\deg\mathsf{d}_{i,k}=k
and
\deg X_{r}=r
for
X=x^{\pm}_{i},\psi_{i}
, the target of
\widetilde{\varrho}
is also filtered as in §
4.4
. It is easy to see that
\widetilde{\varrho}
preserves the filtration, so it induces

\displaystyle\mathrm{gr}\,\widetilde{\varrho}\colon\mathrm{gr}\,Y_{0}(\mathfrak{g}_{A,D})_{\mathrm{loc}}\otimes\mathrm{gr}\,\mathfrak{D}\to\mathrm{gr}\,\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}).

The source of
\mathrm{gr}\,\widetilde{\varrho}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{A,D}[u]\oplus\mathfrak{d}[u])
, where
\mathfrak{d}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{C}(\hbar)\cdot\mathsf{d}_{i}
(see Proposition
4.23
) which we identify
\mathsf{d}_{i}=\mathsf{d}_{i,0}
, and the target of
\mathrm{gr}\,\widetilde{\varrho}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}[u])
. Moreover,
\mathrm{gr}\,\widetilde{\varrho}
is induced from the Lie algebra homomorphism
\mathfrak{g}_{A,D}\oplus\mathfrak{d}\xrightarrow{(\bar{\varrho},\iota)}\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}
, where
\iota
is the natural embedding of
\mathfrak{d}
in
\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\mathsf{W}}
.
Direct computation shows that
\boldsymbol{Q}=-\hbar\,DA
. In particular,
\boldsymbol{Q}
is invertible. It follows that
\mathsf{v}_{i}
is in the linear span of
\mathsf{d}_{i}
and
\{h_{j,0}\}_{j\in\widetilde{Q}_{0}}
. Thus,
(\bar{\varrho},\iota)
is surjective and therefore must be an isomorphism since the two sides have the same dimension. So
\mathrm{gr}\,\widetilde{\varrho}
is an isomorphism. Therefore,
\widetilde{\varrho}
is an isomorphism.
∎

4.7.
Any admissible
\mathcal{C}
is enough

Theorem 4.44
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is admissible, then for any
\mu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}
, we have
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})=\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})
.

Proof.

We first show that
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})=\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
. Notice that
c_{1}(\lambda)\in\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
by Lemma
4.11
, so the coproduct
\Delta c_{1}(\lambda)
is contained in
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})\widehat{\otimes}\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
by Lemma
4.16
. Then (
4.20
) implies that
e_{\alpha}^{(s)}\in\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
for all
\alpha
and
s
; thus
\mathfrak{g}_{\alpha}\subset\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
. The proof of Proposition
4.23
(1) shows that
\mathfrak{g}_{0}\subset\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
. Combine the above two inclusions, and we get
\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}}\subset\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
. Moreover, by Theorem
4.26
, we inductively see that
\mathrm{ch}_{k}(\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{in},i}-\mathsf{D}_{\operatorname{out},i})
is contained in
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
for all
k
, and this implies
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})=\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})
.

By Lemma
4.32
,
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})
is generated by the subset
\bigcup_{a\in\mathbb{C}}\mathsf{S}_{0,\mu;z}(\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W}))\big|_{z=a}
, which is the same as

\bigcup_{a\in\mathbb{C}}\mathsf{S}_{0,\mu;z}(\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C}))\big|_{z=a}\,,

by Step 1, and the latter generates
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})
by Lemma
4.32
again. Thus,
\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W},\mathcal{C})=\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})
.
∎

4.8.
Comparison with Maulik-Okounkov Yangians

Let
Q
be a quiver,
\overline{Q}
be its doubled quiver, and
\widetilde{Q}
be its tripled quiver, equipped with canonical cubic potential (
2.5
):

\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}=\sum_{i\in Q_{0}}\varepsilon_{i}\mu_{i},

where
\varepsilon_{i}
is the edge loop on the node
i
, and
\mu_{i}
is the
i
-th component of moment map. We take
\mathsf{T}_{0}=\mathsf{A}_{0}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\hbar}
, where
\mathsf{A}_{0}
acts on
\overline{Q}
by scaling pair of arrows
(X_{a},X_{a^{*}})
with opposite weights,
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\hbar}
fixes
X_{a}
and scales
X_{a^{*}}
with weight
-1
and scales edge loop
\Phi_{i}
with weight
1
.

Theorem 4.45
.

Conjecture
4.25
is true for a tripled quiver
\widetilde{Q}
with canonical cubic potential
\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}
. Moreover, there is a natural
\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})
-bialgebra isomorphism

\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{\mathsf{W}})\cong\mathsf{Y}^{\mathrm{MO}}_{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0}).

Proof.

Let us take
\mathcal{C}=\{(\delta_{i},\mathsf{T}_{0}\curvearrowright\mathcal{M}(\delta_{i}),\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}_{i})\}_{i\in Q_{0}}
, where
(a,q)\in\mathsf{A}_{0}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\hbar}=\mathsf{T}_{0}
acts on the framing
(A_{i},B_{i})
by

\displaystyle(A_{i},B_{i})\mapsto(A_{i},q^{-1}B_{i}),

and the framed potentials are given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}_{i}=\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}+B_{i}\Phi_{i}A_{i}\>.

It is clear that
\mathcal{C}
is admissible. Then it follows from Theorem
4.44
that
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{\mathsf{W}})\cong\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\mathcal{C})
.

Plug
\mathcal{H}_{0}=\mathcal{H}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}}_{\underline{\mathbf{0}}}=\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]
and
\mathcal{H}_{i}=\mathcal{H}^{\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}_{i}}_{\underline{\delta_{i}}}
into Lemma
4.37
, and the natural map

\displaystyle\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{\mathsf{W}})=\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{\mathsf{W}},\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\prod_{i_{1},\cdots,i_{n}}\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})[a_{i_{1}},\ldots,a_{i_{n}}]}\left(\mathcal{H}_{i_{1}}[a_{i_{1}}]\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{H}_{i_{n}}[a_{i_{n}}]\right)

is injective. By dimensional reduction, we have

\displaystyle H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}}(\mathcal{M}(\delta_{i}),\widetilde{\mathsf{w}}_{i})\cong H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}}(\mathcal{N}(\delta_{i})),

where
\mathcal{N}(\delta_{i})
is the Nakajima variety associated to
\overline{Q}
(Example
2.4
). The above isomorphisms are compatible with stable envelopes (
[
COZZ1
, Thm. 6.10]
) and therefore compatible with
R
-matrices. Thus the image of
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{\mathsf{W}})
in the product of endomorphisms is generated by matrices elements of the
R
-matrices for Nakajima varieties, which agrees with
\mathsf{Y}^{\mathrm{MO}}_{Q}
after localization (since we define
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{\mathsf{W}})
only after localization), that is,

\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{\mathsf{W}})\cong\mathsf{Y}^{\mathrm{MO}}_{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})
. This is a bialgebra isomorphism because the counit is induced by restriction to trivial representation
\mathcal{H}_{0}
, and the coproduct is induced by stable envelope which is compatible with dimensional reduction
(
[
COZZ1
, Thm. 6.10]
).

The Yangian isomorphism is compatible with filtration by degree in
u
, so we get an induced isomorphism on the
F_{0}
part:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}})\cong\mathcal{U}(\mathfrak{g}_{Q}^{\mathrm{MO}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})),

from which we deduce
\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}}\cong\mathfrak{g}_{Q}^{\mathrm{MO}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})
.
Then
[
BD
, Thm. B]
yields

\mathfrak{g}^{+}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}}\cong\mathfrak{g}_{\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{\mathsf{W}}}^{\mathrm{BPS},\mathsf{T}_{0}}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0}).\qed

Corollary 4.46
.

Let
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{d})=\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{v}}\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
be the Nakajima variety associated to a doubled quiver
\overline{Q}
, and let
\mathsf{T}_{0}=\mathbb{C}^{*}_{\hbar}\times\mathsf{S}
act on
\mathrm{Rep}_{Q}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
by flavour symmetry (i.e. commutes with
G=\prod_{i\in Q_{0}}\mathrm{GL}(\mathbf{v}_{i})
action), then
\mathsf{T}_{0}
act on
\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{d})
using the identification
T^{*}\mathrm{Rep}_{Q}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})=\mathrm{Rep}_{Q}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})\oplus\hbar\>\mathrm{Rep}_{Q}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})^{\vee}
. Then for any
\mathsf{T}_{0}
-invariant function
\phi=\operatorname{tr}(\mathsf{m})
(
\mathsf{m}
is a potential of the framed doubled quiver),
H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{d}),\phi)_{\mathrm{loc}}
has a natural
\mathsf{Y}^{\mathrm{MO}}_{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})
module structure.

Moreover, there is a specialization map
[
COZZ1
, Ex. 7.19]
:

(4.24)

\displaystyle\mathsf{sp}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{d}),\phi)_{\mathrm{loc}}\to H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{d}))_{\mathrm{loc}},

which is a
\mathsf{Y}^{\mathrm{MO}}_{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})
module map.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{d})=\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{v}}\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d})
be the symmetric quiver variety associated with the tripled quiver
\widetilde{Q}
, then we have the deformed dimensional reduction isomorphism (
2.6
):

H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{d}),\widetilde{\mathsf{w}}+\phi)\cong H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}}(\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{d}),\phi),

and the former is naturally a
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(\widetilde{Q},\widetilde{\mathsf{W}})\cong\mathsf{Y}^{\mathrm{MO}}_{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})
module. Since (
4.24
) is compatible with stable envelope
[
COZZ1
, Ex. 7.19]
, it commutes with
R
-matrices and therefore induces a
\mathsf{Y}^{\mathrm{MO}}_{Q}\otimes_{\mathbb{C}[\mathsf{t}_{0}]}\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{t}_{0})
module map.
∎

4.9.
Casimir operator

Definition 4.47
.

For any
\alpha\gtrless 0
, define the
Casimir operator

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha}:=\sum_{s}e^{(s)}_{\alpha}e^{(s)}_{-\alpha}\in U(\mathfrak{g}_{Q,\mathsf{W}})\subset\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})

Note that
\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha}-(-1)^{|\alpha|}\mathrm{Cas}_{-\alpha}
is in the Cartan subalgebra
\mathfrak{g}_{0}
by Proposition
4.23
(8).

The Casimir operator, when acting on a state space, is induced by a Steinberg correspondence (Definition
2.17
).

Proposition 4.48
.

Let
\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}}=\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out}}
be a symmetric framing, and denote
X=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},{\mathbf{d}}),X_{0}=\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathbf{v},{\mathbf{d}})
, and
\mathsf{T}=\mathsf{T}_{0}\times\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{fr}}
. Then for any
\alpha>0
, there exists a Steinberg correspondence
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}\in H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim X-2}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
, such that for any potential
\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}}
, the action of
\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha}
on
H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})
is induced by
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Let
i\in Q_{0}
be a node such that
\alpha_{i}>0
. Take an auxiliary symmetric quiver variety by setting the framing dimension to be
\delta_{i}
, let
\mathsf{T}_{0}
act on the framing vector spaces trivially, and introduce an extra torus
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}
such that it only acts on the out-going framing with weight
-1
(and fixes all other arrows). Consider the composition

H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{0},\delta_{i}),\mathsf{w})\otimes H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{r}_{\alpha,-\alpha}}H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(\mathcal{M}(\alpha,\delta_{i}),\mathsf{w})\otimes H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}-\alpha,\mathbf{d}),\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})\\
\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{r}_{-\alpha,\alpha}}H^{\mathsf{T}_{0}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{0},\delta_{i}),\mathsf{w})\otimes H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})\,,

where
\boldsymbol{r}_{\alpha,-\alpha}
and
\boldsymbol{r}_{-\alpha,\alpha}
are the corresponding components of the classical
R
-matrix (
4.12
). By explicit computations,

(4.25)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{r}_{-\alpha,\alpha}\circ\boldsymbol{r}_{\alpha,-\alpha}=\alpha_{i}\,t\,\sum_{s}e^{(s)}_{\alpha}e^{(s)}_{-\alpha}=\alpha_{i}\,t\,\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha}\,.

On the other hand, off-diagonal component of
\boldsymbol{r}
is induced by critical convolution using
\operatorname{can}(\boldsymbol{\rho})
, where
\boldsymbol{\rho}
is a class in
H^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}\left((\mathcal{M}(\delta_{i})\times\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}))\times_{\mathcal{M}_{0}(\delta_{i})\times\mathcal{M}_{0}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})}(\mathcal{M}(\delta_{i})\times\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}))\right)
given by (
2.18
). Therefore
\boldsymbol{r}_{-\alpha,\alpha}\circ\boldsymbol{r}_{\alpha,-\alpha}
is induced by the class
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{-\alpha,\alpha}\circ\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\alpha,-\alpha}\in H^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
of homological degree
2\dim X-4
. Since
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}
acts on
X\times_{X_{0}}X
trivially,

H^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)\cong H^{\mathsf{T}}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}H_{\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(\operatorname{pt}),

and accordingly

(4.26)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\rho}_{-\alpha,\alpha}\circ\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\alpha,-\alpha}=\sum_{k=0}^{2}a_{k}t^{k}\,,\quad a_{k}\in H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim X-2k}(X\times_{X_{0}}X).

Compare (
4.25
) with (
4.26
), and we see that
\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha}
is induced by the class
a_{1}/\alpha_{i}\in H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim X-2}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
. Denote
Y=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\delta_{i}}+\underline{\mathbf{d}})
, let
\pi\colon Y\to B
be the flat morphism to the affine space
B
in
[
COZZ2
]
5
5

5

Denoted
\tau\colon X\to\mathcal{H}/\Gamma
in
[
COZZ2
]
.
, and let
U\subset B
be the dense open subset such that
\pi|_{\pi^{-1}(U)}\colon\pi^{-1}(U)\to U
is an affine morphism. Since
\pi^{-1}(U)
is quasi-affine, so any attracting set is closed in
\pi^{-1}(U)
.
Therefore for two distinct
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{u}
-fixed components
F^{\prime},F\subset Y^{\mathbb{C}^{*}_{u}}
, we have

\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\pm}(\Delta_{F})\cap(\pi^{-1}(U)\times\pi^{-1}(U))=\operatorname{Attr}_{\pm}(\Delta_{F})\cap(\pi^{-1}(U)\times\pi^{-1}(U)),

so the intersection
\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\pm}(\Delta_{F})\cap(F^{\prime}\times F)
is disjoint from
\pi^{-1}(U)\times\pi^{-1}(U)
.
We conclude that, after passing to
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}
-equivariant cohomology, the class
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{-\alpha,\alpha}\circ\boldsymbol{\rho}_{\alpha,-\alpha}
as well as its
t
-expansion coefficients
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}
, are in the image of the pushforward map
H^{\mathbb{C}^{*}_{u}}(X\times_{X_{0}}X\times_{B}(B\setminus U))\to H^{\mathbb{C}^{*}_{u}}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
. In particular,
a_{1}
is a Steinberg correspondence by Lemma
2.20
.
\boldsymbol{\rho}
is independent of potential by construction (
2.18
), so is
a_{1}
.
∎

4.10.
Shifted Casimir operator

Let
\mu<0
be an antidominant shift. Pick
\underline{\mathbf{d}}
with
\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{out}}-\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}}=\mu
, then the symmetric quiver variety
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}})
is the total space of a vector bundle
E=\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{0}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathsf{V}_{i},\mathbb{C}^{-\mu_{i}})
on
\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})
. Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}})\to\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})
be the bundle map. Take a framed potential
\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}}
on
\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})
and its pullback to
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}})
is still denoted by
\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}}
.
Consider the following auxiliary
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}
action on
\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}})
: it acts trivially on
\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})
and scales the fibers of
E
with weight
1
. Then

\displaystyle H^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}}(\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}),\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})\cong H^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}),\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbb{C}[z],

and
\pi^{*}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}}(\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}}),\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})\to H^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}}(\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}}),\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})
is an isomorphism whose inverse is
i^{*}
, where
i\colon\mathcal{M}(\underline{\mathbf{d}})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}})
is the zero section.

By Lemma
4.27
, the assignment
\mathsf{E}(m)\mapsto i^{*}\mathsf{E}(m)_{z}\pi^{*}
induces an algebra homomorphism
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})\to\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})[z]
.

Proposition 4.49
.

Let
\alpha>0
. Under the map
\mathsf{Y}_{0}(Q,\mathsf{W})\to\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})[z]
sending
\mathsf{E}(m)
to
i^{*}\mathsf{E}(m)_{z}\pi^{*}
, the operator
\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha}
is mapped to a polynomial in
z
of degree
\leqslant-\alpha\cdot\mu
.

Moreover, denote
X=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}),X_{0}=\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}})
, then there exists a class
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha,\mu}\in H^{\mathsf{T}}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
, such that for any potential
\mathsf{W}^{\mathrm{fr}}
, the action of the coefficient of
z^{-\alpha\cdot\mu}
in
i^{*}(\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha})_{z}\pi^{*}
on
H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})
is induced by
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha,\mu}
.

Proof.

Let
i\in Q_{0}
be a node such that
\alpha_{i}>0
, and
\underline{\delta_{i}}:=(\delta_{i},\delta_{i})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}^{Q_{0}}
.
Denote

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\delta_{i}}+\underline{\mathbf{d}}),

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{0},\delta_{i})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\mathbf{d}}),

\displaystyle F^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}(\alpha,\delta_{i})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}-\alpha,\underline{\mathbf{d}}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\delta_{i}+\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{0},\delta_{i})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{F}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}(\alpha,\delta_{i})\times\mathcal{M}(\mathbf{v}-\alpha,\mathbf{d}_{\operatorname{in}}).

Then
\widetilde{Y}
is the total space of the vector bundle
E=\bigoplus_{i\in Q_{0}}\operatorname{Hom}(\mathsf{V}_{i},\mathbb{C}^{-\mu_{i}})
.
F,F^{\prime}
belongs to the set of connected components of torus fixed point set
Y^{\mathbb{C}^{*}_{u}}
, where
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{u}
act on the framing by weight decomposition
u\,\underline{\delta_{i}}+\underline{\mathbf{d}}
. This
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{u}
action naturally extends to
\widetilde{Y}
, and under the bundle projection map
\pi\colon\widetilde{Y}\to Y
,
\widetilde{F},\widetilde{F}^{\prime}
are the connected components of
\widetilde{Y}^{\mathbb{C}^{*}_{u}}
that map to
F,F^{\prime}
, respectively. Denote the stable envelopes in the
\pm
-chambers on
Y
(resp. on
\widetilde{Y}
) by
\operatorname{Stab}_{\pm,Y}
(resp.
\operatorname{Stab}_{\pm,\widetilde{Y}}
).

As in the proof of Proposition
4.48
, we let
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}
act on the out-going part of the framing
\underline{\delta_{i}}
with weight
-1
and fix all the other arrows. Then
\mathrm{Cas_{\alpha}}
is determined by the classical
R
-matrix:

\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(\widetilde{F},\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{r}_{\alpha,-\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\alpha_{i}\,t\,\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha}}

=

\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(\widetilde{F}^{\prime},\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{r}_{-\alpha,\alpha}}
\textstyle{H^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(\widetilde{F},\mathsf{w}^{\mathrm{fr}})\,.}

Let
i\colon Y\to\widetilde{Y}
be the zero section map. And we let
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}
act on
\widetilde{Y}
such that it fixes
Y
and scale the fibers of
E
with weight
1
. We claim that

(1)

\operatorname{Stab}_{-,Y}\big|_{F^{\prime}\times F}=\pm u^{\operatorname{rk}N^{+}_{F^{\prime}/Y}-1}\cdot i^{*}\boldsymbol{r}_{\alpha,-\alpha}\pi^{*}+\text{lower order in $u$}
, where the sign is determined by the normalizer
\epsilon
,

(2)

z^{-\alpha\cdot\mu}\operatorname{Stab}_{+,Y}\big|_{F\times F^{\prime}}+\text{lower order in $z$}=\pm u^{\operatorname{rk}N^{-}_{F/Y}-1}\cdot i^{*}\boldsymbol{r}_{-\alpha,\alpha}\pi^{*}+\text{lower order in $u$}
, where the sign is determined by the normalizer
\epsilon
.

Let us assume these claims for now. By
[
COZZ1
, Thm. 8.9]
, the stable envelopes for
Y
are induced by
\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{\pm}(\Delta_{Y^{\mathbb{C}^{*}_{u}}})
. Write

\displaystyle(F^{\prime}\times F\hookrightarrow Y\times F)^{!}[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{-}(\Delta_{F})]=u^{\operatorname{rk}N^{+}_{F^{\prime}/Y}-1}\cdot C_{F^{\prime},F}+\text{lower order in $u$},

\displaystyle(F\times F^{\prime}\hookrightarrow Y\times F^{\prime})^{!}[\overline{\operatorname{Attr}}_{+}(\Delta_{F^{\prime}})]=u^{\operatorname{rk}N^{-}_{F/Y}-1}\cdot C_{F,F^{\prime}}+\text{lower order in $u$},

where
C_{F^{\prime},F}\in H^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(F^{\prime}\times_{Y_{0}}F)
and
C_{F,F^{\prime}}\in H^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(F\times_{Y_{0}}F^{\prime})
with
Y_{0}=\mathcal{M}_{0}(\mathbf{v},\underline{\delta_{i}}+\underline{\mathbf{d}})
. It follows that

\displaystyle i^{*}\boldsymbol{r}_{\alpha,-\alpha}\pi^{*}=\pm C_{F^{\prime},F},\quad i^{*}\boldsymbol{r}_{-\alpha,\alpha}\pi^{*}=\pm z^{-\alpha\cdot\mu}C_{F,F^{\prime}}+\text{lower order in $z$}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\,t\cdot i^{*}(\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha})_{z}\pi^{*}=\pm z^{-\alpha\cdot\mu}C_{F,F^{\prime}}\circ C_{F^{\prime},F}+\text{lower order in $z$}.

This shows that
i^{*}(\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha})_{z}\pi^{*}
is a polynomial in
z
of degree
\leqslant-\alpha\cdot\mu
.

Finally, since
\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}
acts on
F
trivially,
H^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(F\times_{Y_{0}}F)=H^{\mathsf{T}}(F\times_{Y_{0}}F)\otimes H_{\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(\operatorname{pt})
. Expand
C_{F,F^{\prime}}\circ C_{F^{\prime},F}
into a polynomial in
t
:

\displaystyle C_{F,F^{\prime}}\circ C_{F^{\prime},F}=\sum_{n}b_{n}t^{t},\;\text{ with }\;b_{n}\in H^{\mathsf{T}}(F\times_{Y_{0}}F),

then we see that the coefficient of
z^{-\alpha\cdot\mu}
in
i^{*}(\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha})_{z}\pi^{*}
is induced by
\pm b_{1}/\alpha_{i}
. Obviously
b_{1}
does not depend on the potential.

Let us prove the claims. The claim (1) is a consequence of
[
COZZ1
, Prop. 8.2]
, namely, the bundle
E
is attracting in the “
-
” chamber, so we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{Stab}_{-,Y}\big|_{F^{\prime}\times F}=i^{*}\operatorname{Stab}_{-,\widetilde{Y}}\big|_{\widetilde{F}^{\prime}\times\widetilde{F}}\;\pi^{*}=\pm u^{\operatorname{rk}N^{+}_{\widetilde{F}^{\prime}/\widetilde{Y}}-1}\cdot i^{*}\boldsymbol{r}_{\alpha,-\alpha}\pi^{*}+\text{lower order in $u$}.

Since
\operatorname{rk}N^{+}_{\widetilde{F}^{\prime}/\widetilde{Y}}=\operatorname{rk}N^{+}_{F^{\prime}/Y}
, claim (1) follows. The claim (2) is a consequence of
[
COZZ1
, Prop. 8.3]
, namely, the bundle
E
is repelling in the “
+
” chamber, so we have

(4.27)

\begin{split}e^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(E|_{F})\cdot\operatorname{Stab}_{+,Y}\big|_{F\times F^{\prime}}\;\cdot e^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(E|_{F^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{fixed}})^{-1}&=i^{*}\operatorname{Stab}_{+,\widetilde{Y}}\big|_{\widetilde{F}\times\widetilde{F}^{\prime}}\;\pi^{*}\\
&=\pm u^{\operatorname{rk}N^{-}_{\widetilde{F}/\widetilde{Y}}-1}\cdot i^{*}\boldsymbol{r}_{-\alpha,\alpha}\pi^{*}+\text{lower order in $u$}.\end{split}

Note that
\operatorname{rk}N^{-}_{\widetilde{F}/\widetilde{Y}}=\operatorname{rk}N^{-}_{F/Y}
. The equivariant Euler classes are given by

\displaystyle e^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(E|_{F})=z^{-\mathbf{v}\cdot\mu}+\text{lower order in $z$},\quad e^{\mathsf{T}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{z}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}_{t}}(E|_{F^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{fixed}})=z^{-(\mathbf{v}-\alpha)\cdot\mu}+\text{lower order in $z$\,,}

and they are independent of
u
. Thus, the LHS of (
4.27
) in the
z
-expansion is
z^{-\alpha\cdot\mu}\operatorname{Stab}_{+,Y}\big|_{F\times F^{\prime}}+\text{lower order in $z$}
, and the claim (2) follows.
∎

Definition 4.50
.

For
\alpha>0
, we define the
shifted Casimir operator

\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha,\mu}\in\mathsf{Y}_{\mu}(Q,\mathsf{W})
to be the coefficient of
z^{-\alpha\cdot\mu}
in
(-1)^{\alpha\cdot\mu}\,i^{*}(\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha})_{z}\pi^{*}
, equivalently,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha,\mu}=\lim_{z\to\infty}(-z)^{\alpha\cdot\mu}\cdot i^{*}(\mathrm{Cas}_{\alpha})_{z}\pi^{*}\,.

5.
Applications to quantum multiplication by divisors

Building on techniques developed in the previous section, we
deduce the formula of quantum multiplication by divisors for symmetric quiver varieties with potentials (Theorem
5.10
),
and certain asymmetric cases (Theorem
5.13
).

5.1.
Quantum multiplication by divisors

Let
X
be a quiver variety (
2.2
) with a
\mathsf{T}
-invariant function
\mathsf{w}
, and the affinization map
X\to X_{0}
. Consider the moduli stack of
genus
0
, 3-pointed stable maps with proper evaluation map

\operatorname{ev}:=\operatorname{ev}_{1}\times\operatorname{ev}_{2}\times\operatorname{ev}_{3}\colon\overline{M}_{0,3}(X,\beta)\to X\times X\times X.

As any domain curve of a stable map contracts to a point in the affine space
X_{0}
, we have

\operatorname{ev}(\overline{M}_{0,3}(X,\beta))\subseteq(X\times Z(\mathsf{w}\boxminus\mathsf{w}))\cap(X\times_{X_{0}}X\times_{X_{0}}X).

As
X
is smooth, there is a
\mathsf{T}
-equivariant virtual class

[\overline{M}_{0,3}(X,\beta)]_{\mathsf{T}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\in H^{\mathsf{T}}(\overline{M}_{0,3}(X,\beta)).

Definition 5.1
.

Given any
\gamma\in H_{\mathsf{T}}^{*}(X)
, we define

(5.1)

M_{\beta}(\gamma):=\mathrm{can}\circ(\operatorname{ev}_{2}\times\operatorname{ev}_{3})_{*}([\overline{M}_{0,3}(X,\beta)]_{\mathsf{T}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\cap\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{*}\gamma)\in H^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{2},\mathsf{w}\boxminus\mathsf{w}).

This induces the
quantum multiplication
by classes in
\gamma\in H_{\mathsf{T}}^{*}(X)
:

(5.2)

\gamma\star_{\beta}\cdot\,\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})\to H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w}),\quad\alpha\mapsto M_{\beta}(\gamma)\circ\alpha,

where
\circ
is the convolution product
[
COZZ1
, §3.4.1]
. Summing over
\beta
, we define

(5.3)

\gamma\star\cdot:=\sum_{\beta}(\gamma\star_{\beta}\cdot)\,z^{\beta}\,\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})[\![z]\!]\to H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})[\![z]\!].

We define

\mathrm{Q}_{\beta}:=\mathrm{can}\circ\operatorname{ev}_{*}([\overline{M}_{0,2}(X,\beta)]_{\mathsf{T}}^{\mathrm{vir}})\in H^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{2},\mathsf{w}\boxminus\mathsf{w})_{X\times_{X_{0}}X}.

By abuse of notations, its induced map (via convolution) is denoted by

(5.4)

\mathrm{Q}_{X,\beta}=\mathrm{Q}_{\beta}\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})\to H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w}).

Summing over
\beta
, we define

(5.5)

\mathrm{Q}_{X}=\mathrm{Q}:=\sum_{\beta}\mathrm{Q}_{\beta}\,z^{\beta}\,\colon H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})[\![z]\!]\to H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})[\![z]\!].

Lemma 5.2
.

The map
\gamma\mapsto\gamma\star\cdot
is unital and associative with respect to the quantum product on
H^{*}_{\mathsf{T}}(X)
, i.e.

1\star\alpha=\alpha,\quad\gamma_{1}\star(\gamma_{2}\star\alpha)=(\gamma_{1}\star\gamma_{2})\star\alpha,\quad\forall\,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in H^{*}_{\mathsf{T}}(X),\,\alpha\in H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})[\![z]\!].

In other words,
\gamma\mapsto\gamma\star\cdot
induces a quantum cohomology
QH^{*}_{\mathsf{T}}(X)
module structure on
H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})[\![z]\!]
.

Proof.

\operatorname{ev}_{2}\times\operatorname{ev}_{3}\colon\overline{M}_{0,3}(X,\beta)\to X^{2}
factors through the universal curve map
\overline{M}_{0,3}(X,\beta)\to\overline{M}_{0,2}(X,\beta)
, so

(\operatorname{ev}_{2}\times\operatorname{ev}_{3})_{*}([\overline{M}_{0,3}(X,\beta)]_{\mathsf{T}}^{\mathrm{vir}})=0.

It follows that
1\star\cdot=\operatorname{id}
.

By a standard degeneration argument, we have the following identity in
H^{\mathsf{T}}(X^{2},\mathsf{w}\boxminus\mathsf{w})
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{can}\circ(\operatorname{ev}_{3}\times\operatorname{ev}_{4})_{*}([\overline{M}_{0,4}(X,\beta)]_{\mathsf{T}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\cap\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{*}\gamma_{1}\cap\operatorname{ev}_{2}^{*}\gamma_{2})=\sum_{\beta_{1}+\beta_{2}=\beta}M_{\beta_{1}}(\gamma_{1})\circ M_{\beta_{2}}(\gamma_{2}).

Degeneration in another way gives

(\operatorname{ev}_{3}\times\operatorname{ev}_{4})_{*}([\overline{M}_{0,4}(X,\beta)]_{\mathsf{T}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\cap\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{*}\gamma_{1}\cap\operatorname{ev}_{2}^{*}\gamma_{2})\\
=\sum_{\beta^{\prime}_{1}+\beta^{\prime}_{2}=\beta}\operatorname{pr}_{45*}i_{3}^{*}\left(\operatorname{ev}_{3*}([\overline{M}_{0,3}(X,\beta_{1}^{\prime})]_{\mathsf{T}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\cap\operatorname{ev}_{1}^{*}\gamma_{1}\cap\operatorname{ev}_{2}^{*}\gamma_{2})\boxtimes(\operatorname{ev}_{3}\times\operatorname{ev}_{4}\times\operatorname{ev}_{5})_{*}([\overline{M}_{0,3}(X,\beta_{2}^{\prime})]_{\mathsf{T}}^{\mathrm{vir}})\right),

where
i_{3}\colon X_{3}\times X_{4}\times X_{5}\hookrightarrow X_{3}\times X_{3}\times X_{4}\times X_{5}
is induced by the diagonal of
X_{3}
, and
\operatorname{pr}_{45}\colon X_{3}\times X_{4}\times X_{5}\to X_{4}\times X_{5}
is the projection. Applying canonical map and summing over
\beta
gives

\gamma_{1}\star\cdot\circ\gamma_{2}\star\cdot=\gamma_{1}\cap\gamma_{2}\cap\cdot+\sum_{\beta_{2}^{\prime}}\operatorname{can}\circ(\operatorname{ev}_{4}\times\operatorname{ev}_{5})_{*}([\overline{M}_{0,3}(X,\beta_{2}^{\prime})]_{\mathsf{T}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\cap\operatorname{ev}_{3}^{*}(\gamma_{1}\star\gamma_{2}))=(\gamma_{1}\star\gamma_{2})\star\cdot\;.\qed

Lemma 5.3
.

The class
\operatorname{ev}_{*}([\overline{M}_{0,2}(X,\beta)]_{\mathsf{T}}^{\mathrm{vir}})\in H^{\mathsf{T}}_{2\dim X-2}(X\times_{X_{0}}X)
is a Steinberg correspondence in the sense of Definition
2.17
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.20
, it suffices to show that the image of
\operatorname{ev}\colon\overline{M}_{0,2}(X,\beta)\to X\times_{X_{0}}X
is contained in
X\times_{X_{0}}X\times_{B}(B\setminus U)
, and this follows from the fact that
B
is affine and
\pi|_{\pi^{-1}(U)}\colon\pi^{-1}(U)\to U
is an affine morphism (so any proper curve is mapped to
\pi^{-1}(B\setminus U)
).
∎

Remark 5.4
.

If
\gamma
is a divisor class,
\beta
is a nonzero curve class, divisor equation implies that

M_{\beta}(\gamma)=(L\cdot\beta)\cdot\mathrm{Q}_{\beta}.

In general, there is a conjecture of Joyce, see e.g.
[
Joy
,
CZ
,
CTZ
]
, asserting that by counting certain ‘twisted’ maps with
r
-marked points (assuming
\mathrm{Crit}(\mathsf{w})^{\mathsf{T}}
is proper), one obtains an

r
-pointed function:

(5.6)

H^{\mathsf{T}}(X,\mathsf{w})^{\otimes r}\to H_{\mathsf{T}}(\operatorname{pt})_{\mathrm{loc}}.

In the two marked point case (
r=2
), we can choose the twist to be trivial and
the twisted maps in
loc. cit.
become ordinary maps from rational curves
C
such that
\omega_{\log,C}\cong\mathcal{O}_{C}
.
Under Verdier duality,
the provisional two pointed function (
5.6
) becomes an operator as (
5.4
), we expect it coincides with
\mathrm{Q}_{\beta}
up to a scalar operator.

5.2.
Stable envelopes v.s. operator
\mathrm{Q}

By
[
COZZ1
, Lem. 3.29]
,
\operatorname{Stab}_{\mathfrak{C}}^{-1}
is induced by the transpose of the stable envelope correspondence for the opposite chamber, i.e.
